```ts
// a folder under `modelRoot(context)`. Nothing here hard-codes an install path, so `temcoAi.modelFolder`
// moves the server with everything else.

import * as vscode from 'vscode';
import { CatalogEntry, toolServerFor } from './catalog';
import { install, installedArtifacts } from './install';

/** The catalog entry for this machine, if the catalogue has one for it. */
export function serverEntry(): CatalogEntry | undefined {
  return toolServerFor(process.platform, process.arch);
}

/** The installed executable, or `undefined` when it has not been installed on this machine yet. */
export function serverBinary(context: vscode.ExtensionContext): string | undefined {
  const entry = serverEntry();
  if (!entry) {
    return undefined;
  }
  return installedArtifacts(context).find((artifact) => artifact.id === entry.id)?.path;
}

/** Installs it if it is missing, and answers with the executable path. */
export async function ensureServerBinary(context: vscode.ExtensionContext): Promise<string> {
  const entry = serverEntry();
  if (!entry) {
    throw new Error(
      `No tool server is published for ${process.platform}-${process.arch}, so the assistant cannot call anything here.`
    );
  }
  const artifact = await install(context, entry);
  return artifact.path;
}

/** What the server is told about this machine. Nothing else is inherited. */
export function serverEnv(context: vscode.ExtensionContext): Record<string, string> {
  const env: Record<string, string> = {};
  for (const key of ['PATH', 'HOME', 'USERPROFILE', 'SystemRoot', 'TEMP', 'TMP', 'TMPDIR', 'LANG']) {
    const value = process.env[key];
    if (typeof value === 'string') {
      env[key] = value;
    }
  }
  env.TEMCO_CATALOG_DB = vscode.Uri.joinPath(
    context.globalStorageUri,
    'catalog',
    'catalog.db'
  ).fsPath;
  env.TEMCO_MCP_STATE = context.globalStorageUri.fsPath;
  env.TEMCO_PLATFORM_API = platformApi();
  return env;
}

/** The address the device, repository and project tools ask. Empty means they are not usable here. */
export function platformApi(): string {
  return vscode.workspace.getConfiguration('temcoAi').get<string>('platformApi', '').trim();
}
```

Three consequences, all of them wanted:

- The installer stays the size it is; the server arrives on first use, the same way a model does, and asks once before it does.
- `ArtifactKind` in `catalog.ts:7` becomes `'runtime' | 'model' | 'tool-server'`, and one `install()` serves all three. `modelRoot(context)` stays the single root, so `temcoAi.modelFolder` moves the server too.
- A missing binary is an ordinary state, not an error: the Tool Server page's **Start** downloads and starts it, and until then the plain answer path runs unchanged.

#### 6.4 `ai/agent.ts`, the loop

The loop keeps its shape — ask, run what it asked for, ask again, with a six-step budget and a set of calls that need a person's yes. What changed is where the server comes from: it is not opened and disposed per turn any more, because it is a process the window owns and the Tool Server page shows.

```ts
import { platformApi, serverBinary } from './binPath';
import { ChatMessage, ToolSpec, complete } from './runtime';
import { callThroughServer, toolServer } from './server';

/** The tools the server offers, without asking the model anything. Starts the server when it is not up. */
export async function listTools(context: vscode.ExtensionContext): Promise<readonly ToolSpec[]> {
  return toolServer().tools(context);
}

export async function callTool(
  context: vscode.ExtensionContext,
  name: string,
  args: Record<string, unknown>,
  options: { log?: (line: string) => void; approve?: Approval; token?: vscode.CancellationToken } = {}
): Promise<string> {
  if (needsApproval(name)) {
    const yes = options.approve ? await options.approve(name, args) : false;
    if (!yes) {
      options.log?.(`[mcp] ${name} declined`);
      return 'The person at the keyboard declined this call.';
    }
  }
  return callThroughServer(context, name, args, options.token);
}

export async function runAgent(context, messages, options): Promise<AgentResult> {
  // No platform service is no longer fatal: the catalog tools answer from this machine.
  if (platformApi().length === 0) {
    options.log('[mcp] no platform service is configured (temcoAi.platformApi); tools that need one will say so');
  }

  const tools = await listTools(context);
  for (let step = 0; step < MAX_STEPS; step++) {
    const reply = await complete(context, thread, { tools, token: options.token });
    // ... unchanged: push the assistant turn, gate each call, run it through callThroughServer, push the result
  }
}
```

Three properties are worth naming, because each was a decision:

- **Both entry points still pass one gate.** `callTool()` asks before a write, and `runAgent` asks before a write, and both are in this file so a third caller cannot arrive without one.
- **The approval set stays in the extension.** It is not read from the server's `destructiveHint`, which a tampered server could set to `false`.
- **A missing platform service is a sentence, not a throw.** It used to stop the turn; now the catalog tools answer and the rest say the service did not answer, which is something the model can pass on to a person.


#### 6.5 Lifecycle

`ai/server.ts` owns the process. It is the one place that starts it, so the panel, the Tools page's runner and the Tool Server page all talk to the same thing.

| Event | What happens |
| --- | --- |
| Nothing needs the server | It is not running. Opening a page never starts it, and `status()` says `stopped` |
| A turn, or the Tools page, needs the catalogue | `toolServer().tools(context)` calls `ensure()`, which starts it if it is not up and reuses the process if it is |
| First start on a machine with no server | `start()` downloads it through `install()`, then starts it — "Start" is the button that installs |
| Port | `temcoAi.serverPort`, or a free port found by binding one and releasing it. Never a fixed default: two windows must not collide |
| Token | 24 random bytes per start, passed in `TEMCO_MCP_TOKEN`, required on every request but `/health`. It is not a secret to be stored: it dies with the process |
| Readiness | `/health` is polled every 250 ms for up to 30 s. Only a 200 counts; a process that is up but silent is not running |
| The process exits on its own | State `failed`, with the last three lines it wrote, so the page can say what happened instead of "stopped" |
| `temcoAi.serverIdleMinutes` | `0` (the default) keeps it for the window. Anything else stops it after that long without a call |
| The window closes | `deactivate()` calls `disposeToolServer()`, which kills the child. Nothing is left behind |
| No artifact for this platform | `serverEntry()` is undefined, `start()` fails with that sentence, and the plain answer path still runs |


---

### 7. Building it

#### 7.1 Locally

```powershell
winget install Microsoft.DotNet.SDK.10   # once per machine; .NET 10 is the current LTS

dotnet publish tools/temco-mcp/Temco.Mcp.csproj -c Release -r win-x64 --self-contained true `
  -p:PublishSingleFile=false -o artifacts/temco-mcp-win-x64
dotnet publish tools/temco-mcp/Temco.Mcp.csproj -c Release -r linux-x64 --self-contained true `
  -p:PublishSingleFile=false -o artifacts/temco-mcp-linux-x64
dotnet publish tools/temco-mcp/Temco.Mcp.csproj -c Release -r osx-arm64 --self-contained true `
  -p:PublishSingleFile=false -o artifacts/temco-mcp-osx-arm64
```

The three RIDs are the three rows of the release matrix in `.github/workflows/release.yml`:

| Release matrix | `process.platform` / `process.arch` | RID | Catalog entry id |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `win32` / `x64` | `win32` / `x64` | `win-x64` | `temco-mcp-win-x64` |
| `linux` / `x64` | `linux` / `x64` | `linux-x64` | `temco-mcp-linux-x64` |
| `darwin` / `arm64` | `darwin` / `arm64` | `osx-arm64` | `temco-mcp-osx-arm64` |

`serverEntry()` matches on `process.platform` and `process.arch`, so the table is the only place the mapping is written down; the folder name comes from the entry id, which `install()` uses as the unpack folder.

#### 7.2 What it produces

344 files and 108 MB for one platform, because a self-contained publish carries the runtime with it — and, since the HTTP transport arrived, ASP.NET Core with it. The whole folder is the artifact: apphost, `temco-mcp.dll`, `e_sqlite3.dll`, and the framework. Nothing is written next to it at run time, so it may sit in a read-only store.

#### 7.3 Into an archive, and its checksum

`install()` unpacks with the platform's own `tar`, which reads `.tar.gz` everywhere (GNU tar does not read `.zip`), then finds the executable by name anywhere inside. The archive is the unit that is downloaded and verified, exactly as a model is:

```powershell
tar -czf artifacts/temco-mcp-win-x64.tar.gz -C artifacts temco-mcp-win-x64
(Get-FileHash artifacts/temco-mcp-win-x64.tar.gz -Algorithm SHA256).Hash.ToLowerInvariant()
```

The hash and the byte count go into the `TOOL_SERVERS` entry in `catalog.ts`, with `kind: 'tool-server'`, `platform`, `architecture` and `licence`. **They are not pinned yet**, and until they are, `install()` asks once before it downloads: a modal naming the file, the exact URL, the size (or "not published in the catalog") and the folder it lands in, with one **Download** button. The checksum the file turns out to have is recorded in the manifest either way, so a later change is still noticed. `temcoAi.allowUnpinned` skips the question for a machine that installs unattended — it no longer *is* the question. The unpacked folder `temco-mcp-win-x64` is what `install()` records, and `artifact.path` is the executable inside it.

That is the whole first run, and it was exercised end to end against a local HTTP server serving the real archive: cancel installs nothing; **Download** fetches 49,248,480 bytes, writes the manifest (`id, kind, file, sha256, bytes, url, path, installedAt`) and leaves `temco-mcp.exe` where `serverBinary()` looks; a second install asks nothing; `allowUnpinned` asks nothing.

`.gitignore` carries `tools/temco-mcp/bin/`, `tools/temco-mcp/obj/` and `extensions/temco-ai/bin/`: build output of the machine, never of git.

#### 7.4 In CI

In `.github/workflows/release.yml`, in the build job. The SDK is installed next to Node, and the publish happens after the application is packaged and before the platform's staging step, so the archive lands in the same `staging/` folder the release collects:

```yaml
      - uses: actions/setup-dotnet@v4
        with:
          dotnet-version: 10.0.x

      - name: Stage the tool server
        shell: bash
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          case "${{ matrix.platform }}-${{ matrix.arch }}" in
            win32-x64) rid=win-x64 ;;
            linux-x64) rid=linux-x64 ;;
            darwin-arm64) rid=osx-arm64 ;;
            *) echo "::error::No tool server is published for ${{ matrix.platform }}-${{ matrix.arch }}."; exit 1 ;;
          esac
          dotnet publish tools/temco-mcp/Temco.Mcp.csproj -c Release -r "$rid" --self-contained true \
            -p:PublishSingleFile=false -o "tool-server/$rid"
          mkdir -p staging
          tar -czf "staging/temco-mcp-$rid.tar.gz" -C "tool-server/$rid" .
          ls -l "staging/temco-mcp-$rid.tar.gz"
```

The existing `softprops/action-gh-release` step already collects `release/*.tar.gz`, so the three archives ride in the release next to the installers without a second upload step.

Two properties matter:

1. It runs on the same runner as the build, so the RID matches the artefact the release publishes. There is no cross-compilation and no RID guessing.
2. It is a separate step with its own exit code, so a broken tool server fails the release instead of hiding behind a later command — the failure mode that the Windows `pwsh` steps in this workflow were split to avoid.

The catalogue does not pin the tag. `TOOL_SERVER_RELEASE` in `catalog.ts` is `` `v${vscode.version.split('-')[0]}` ``, because every release attaches its own three archives: a pinned tag would send a newer application to an older build, or to a tag that never carried one.


#### 7.5 During development

```powershell
dotnet publish tools/temco-mcp/Temco.Mcp.csproj -c Release -r win-x64 --self-contained true `
  -p:PublishSingleFile=false -o extensions/temco-ai/bin/win-x64
```

That folder is the third place `serverBinary()` looks, and it is in `.gitignore`. A source checkout therefore runs the server from its own tree, before any release carries an artifact; a packaged application has it only if the installer put one there, which it does not. The three places are tried in order: what the installer recorded (`installedArtifacts()`), `<modelRoot>/<entry.id>` — which is what `temcoAi.modelFolder` moves, and where a manifest entry makes `installedArtifacts()` see a hand-placed copy — and `extensions/temco-ai/bin/<rid>` beside this extension.

The server answers two ways, and both are testable without the application:

```powershell
# stdio: one request at a time on a pipe, for an inspector or a script
npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector extensions\temco-ai\bin\win-x64\temco-mcp.exe

# HTTP: the mode the application manages
$env:TEMCO_MCP_TRANSPORT='http'; $env:TEMCO_MCP_PORT='7391'; $env:TEMCO_MCP_TOKEN='abc'
extensions\temco-ai\bin\win-x64\temco-mcp.exe
curl.exe http://127.0.0.1:7391/health
#   -> {"ok":true,"name":"temco-mcp","version":"1.0.0"} without a token
curl.exe -X POST http://127.0.0.1:7391/mcp -H "Content-Type: application/json" `
  -H "Accept: application/json, text/event-stream" `
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":1,"method":"tools/list","params":{}}'
#   -> 401 without the header, and the 23 tools with -H "Authorization: Bearer abc"
```


---

### 8. Running it inside the application

#### 8.1 The process tree

```
temco-platform.exe                    the window
 └─ extension host (Node, type: commonjs)
     ├─ llama-server                   ai/runtime.ts:144    one model, loopback HTTP
     └─ temco-mcp                      ai/server.ts         one per window, loopback HTTP, managed
         └─ (HTTP) the platform service on 127.0.0.1:3210
```

Neither child starts at activation. `llama-server` starts on the first `complete()`; `temco-mcp` starts the first time something needs the catalogue — a turn, the Tools page, or the Start button — and then stays until the idle setting or the closing window says otherwise. Both are stopped explicitly, so a closed window leaves no processes behind.


#### 8.2 Where the binary is, per layout

| Layout | Install root | Executable |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Development | `<repo>/extensions/temco-ai` `globalStorage`, or `temcoAi.modelFolder` | `<root>/temco-mcp-win-x64/temco-mcp.exe` |
| Packaged | `<userData>/User/globalStorage/temcocontrols.temco-ai` | `<root>/temco-mcp-win-x64/temco-mcp.exe` |
| Source checkout | `extensions/temco-ai/bin/<rid>` beside the extension | `extensions/temco-ai/bin/win-x64/temco-mcp.exe` |

The binary is *not* inside `extensions/temco-ai`, and no `.vscodeignore` question arises: it is installed by `install()`, the same call that installs a model, and resolved by `installedArtifacts()`. The install root is the same root the models use, so a user who moved `temcoAi.modelFolder` onto a larger disk moved the tool server with them.

The published file keeps its executable bit through `Compress-Archive`/`unzip`, and the manifest records the path that was verified. If a copy loses the bit, the first `open()` fails with `EACCES`; the one-time repair belongs next to the spawn:

```ts
if (process.platform !== 'win32') {
  try {
    fs.chmodSync(binary, 0o755);
  } catch {
    // Read-only install; the file most likely already carries the bit.
  }
}
```

#### 8.3 What the application passes in

| Variable | Value | Why the server needs it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `TEMCO_CATALOG_DB` | `temco-store`'s `databasePath()` | The catalog, opened `ReadOnly`, WAL already on. Asked of the module that owns the file, not derived from this extension's own storage |
| `TEMCO_MCP_STATE` | `<globalStorage>` | Where `audit.db` is created |
| `TEMCO_PLATFORM_API` | `temcoAi.platformApi`, default `http://127.0.0.1:3210` | Where the device, repo, and project tools get their answers |
| `TEMCO_MCP_TRANSPORT` | `http` | Which of the two transports to serve. Absent means `stdio` |
| `TEMCO_MCP_PORT` | `temcoAi.serverPort`, or a free port the supervisor found | Where to listen, on `127.0.0.1` only |
| `TEMCO_MCP_TOKEN` | 24 random bytes per start | The bearer token every request but `/health` must carry |
| `PATH`, `HOME`/`USERPROFILE`, `SystemRoot`, `TEMP*`, `LANG` | copied from the extension host | Nothing else is inherited; a curated list is auditable |

Everything else is a setting read with `vscode.workspace.getConfiguration('temcoAi')` in `serverEnv()` and `server.ts`, never a file next to the binary. `TEMCO_MCP_LOG` is honoured if it is set (`information` puts the server's own per-call logging back); the extension leaves it alone, so the channel carries calls rather than the framework's chatter.

#### 8.4 The wire contract

The server speaks both transports, chosen by `TEMCO_MCP_TRANSPORT`; the application uses `http`.

**`stdio`** — newline-delimited JSON-RPC on stdin/stdout, stderr for logs. One rule follows from a failure observed while writing this document: **the client must keep stdin open for the life of the session.** Driving the server from a file that ends immediately produced a full stderr log, executed tool calls, and *zero* responses on stdout. `StdioClientTransport` holds the pipe open; a hand-written probe must too. The server also never writes to stdout outside the protocol, which is why §5.3 sends logs to stderr.

**`http`** — `WebApplication` on `http://127.0.0.1:<TEMCO_MCP_PORT>`, with `WithHttpTransport()` in its default *stateless* mode:

| Route | Behaviour |
| --- | --- |
| `GET /health` | `{"ok":true,"name":"temco-mcp","version":"1.0.0"}`. No token: it is how the supervisor knows the process is up, and it says nothing worth hiding |
| `GET /` and `GET /mcp` | The same facts in words: what this process is, that the protocol is `POST /mcp`, and that the application holds the token. No token, because someone who pasted the address into a browser was going to see *something*, and a bare 401 taught them nothing |
| `POST /mcp` | JSON-RPC. `tools/list` and `tools/call` work as the first request on a connection, because there is no session to establish |
| Any other path | 401 unless `Authorization: Bearer <TEMCO_MCP_TOKEN>` |

Three details the client depends on, each verified against the running server:

1. **No `Mcp-Session-Id`.** Stateless mode rejects the header outright (`400 … not supported in stateless mode`), so the client keeps no session state and one POST is one question.
2. **Answers arrive framed as events.** The content type is `text/event-stream` and the JSON is on one `data:` line, even for a plain request. A client that only tries `JSON.parse(body)` sees an empty answer.
3. **`/health` is not the same thing as ready.** It answers before the tool list does, which is why the supervisor polls it rather than the catalogue.

The listening socket is bound to `127.0.0.1` by name, never to `0.0.0.0`, and the token is generated per start: a loopback port is reachable by every process on the machine, including the three tools that write.

**Pasting the address into a browser** used to give 401 — correct, and useless. It now answers with the description above (`GET /` and `GET /mcp`, verified: 200 with the JSON, while `POST /mcp` without the header still answers 401 and with it answers the 23 tools). The Tool Server page says the same thing in one sentence, so nobody has to discover it from a status code.


#### 8.5 Verifying it end to end

```powershell
# 1. the server answers MCP at all, without the application
npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector <installRoot>\temco-mcp-win-x64\temco-mcp.exe
#    -> the inspector lists the tools from §5.7 and can call one

# 2. the runtime can call a tool at all. Both paths are printed by the extension on start:
#    "[ai] starting <label> as <runtime.path> on 127.0.0.1:<port>" in the "Temco AI" output channel.
Set-Content -LiteralPath probe.json -Encoding UTF8 -Value '{"model":"local","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"read input 1 of device 1234"}],"tools":[{"type":"function","function":{"name":"device.read_point","description":"Read one point: its value, units and range.","parameters":{"type":"object","properties":{"serial":{"type":"integer"},"pointType":{"type":"string"},"pointIndex":{"type":"integer"}},"required":["serial","pointType","pointIndex"]}}}]}'
& "<runtime.path>" --model "<model.path>" --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8099 --jinja
#    in a second window, once the model has loaded:
curl.exe http://127.0.0.1:8099/v1/chat/completions -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@probe.json"
#    -> a working setup answers with choices[0].message.tool_calls, not prose

# 3. the whole turn, in the application
scripts\code.bat
#    -> ask in the Temco AI panel: "read input 1 of device 1234"
#    -> the "Temco AI" output channel shows [mcp] 23 tools from …, then the call, then the answer
```

Step 2 decides whether `--jinja` alone is enough for the chosen model. If the reply is prose with no `tool_calls`, the model's template is the reason, and the answer is `--chat-template-file` or a model whose template the runtime already knows.

#### 8.6 The second exposure: the same server for Copilot Chat

The server is a plain MCP server, so the application can also declare it to the language model that ships with the product — same binary, same environment, started by the editor instead of by `temco-ai`.

Two ways to declare it, and they are different products: `McpStdioServerDefinition` gives the editor its own `stdio` process (the mode is still there, and needs no token because nothing else can reach a pipe), while `McpHttpServerDefinition` points at the managed process — which is only right while it is running, and is why the definition provider below would have to be re-registered when the state changes. The `stdio` one is the simpler contract for a second consumer.

```ts
export function registerToolServer(context: vscode.ExtensionContext): vscode.Disposable {
  return vscode.lm.registerMcpServerDefinitionProvider('temcoAi.tools', {
    provideMcpServerDefinitions: () => {
      const binary = serverBinary(context);
      return binary
        ? [new vscode.McpStdioServerDefinition('Temco Platform Tools', binary, [], serverEnv(context), '1.0.0')]
        : [];
    },
  });
}
```

`McpStdioServerDefinition(label, command, args?, env?, version?)`; `version` is what makes the editor notice a changed tool list and offer a refresh. The provider is called eagerly and must not prompt — anything interactive belongs in `resolveMcpServerDefinition`.

#### 8.7 When it does not start

| Symptom | Cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `spawn … ENOENT` | The artifact is not installed, or the manifest has no entry for this platform | Press Start on the Tool Server page; it installs for this RID (§7.1) |
| `spawn … EACCES` on macOS or Linux | The executable bit was lost in a copy | The `chmod` guard in §8.2 |
| `Failed` with `address already in use` in the log | `temcoAi.serverPort` names a port something else holds | Set it back to `0`, or pick another |
| `did not answer … within 30s` | The process started and never served — a port it could not bind, or a crash before `app.RunAsync()` | The three lines the page shows are the end of stderr; the whole log is behind **Show the log** |
| The page says `failed` with `stopped (exit 1)` | The server exited on its own; the message carries the last lines it wrote | Read them first — an ASP.NET host failure names itself |
| `the tool server refused this window's token` | The process was replaced while a request was in flight (a restart) | Press Restart, or just ask again: the next call starts it |
| `SQLITE_BUSY` | A writer holds the catalog longer than 5 s | The `busy_timeout` in §5.6; a long write in `temco-store` is the real cause |
| The model reads `An error occurred invoking …` | The tool threw instead of returning `CallToolResult` — including a refused connection to the platform service | §5.4 rule 1 |
| Tools listed, calls return prose | The model answered instead of calling | Tighten the tool descriptions; the prompt's instruction to call is the other half |
| `tool_calls` never appear | `--jinja` is missing | §4.1, then §8.5 step 2 |

---

### 9. Left panel and pages

The activity-bar group stays `AI` (`temcoAiHub`, owned by `temco-workbench`); `temco-ai` contributes four group views into it and keeps its own secondary-sidebar view.

| Surface | Id | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Activity bar, `AI` | `temcoAiHub` | Group container |
| - Draft | `temcoAi.draft` | The turn in progress, and what is waiting to be settled |
| - What it does | `temcoAi.whatItDoes` | The acts, and the pages the answers leant on |
| - Server | `temcoAi.server` | The model: runtime, model, and what it is told |
| - Tools | `temcoAi.mcp` | What the assistant can reach outside this window |
| Secondary side bar | `temcoAi.panel` (`Temco AI`) | The conversation |
| Editor page | `temcoAi.page` | Today's settings page: the runtime and its prompts |
| Editor page | `temcoAi.tools` | The catalogue of calls, and the box that runs one |
| Editor page | `temcoAi.connection` | The tool server: its state, its address, Start/Stop/Restart, the port, the platform address, the log |
| Editor pages | `temcoAi.actions`, `temcoAi.citations`, `temcoAi.stale` | Unchanged |

The `Tools` group is the way in. Three rows: the conversation, the connection, and the calls. Two of them open pages of their own, one each.

| Row | Command | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Open the AI panel | `temcoAi.openPanel` | Reveals the conversation; **the same row puts it away.** A plain open toggles, because the row a reader opens a thing with is the row they reach for again. A caller that names a page never closes anything |
| The Tool Server | `temcoAi.connection` | The program behind every call: whether it is running, where it answers, how to stop it, and the two settings it reads |
| The tools it can call | `temcoAi.tools` | The catalogue itself |

The title bar carries the same two ideas as buttons, so the group is useful without opening a page. The log is **not** a fourth row: it is a button on the Tool Server page, where the rest of the program's state is.

Two pages rather than one, because a status and a list are different questions and the first one is asked when something is wrong. A page that answered both put a **Start** button under a catalogue that had to be loaded before the button could be seen — which is exactly the state (nothing is running) in which the catalogue cannot load.

`temcoAi.tools` is a page, not a tree view, because a flat list of rows cannot show an argument schema. Two parts:

- **The catalogue**: tabs, one per part of the API, taken from the first word of each call's name and kept in the order the server lists them, with a count beside each: `Device 8`, `Docs 4`, `Repo 5`. The strip is a plain row of words with their counts: twelve-pixel labels in the section's own face at six hundred weight, the first letter capitalised, no background under the pointer or under the open tab, and a single hairline in the theme's primary colour along the bottom of the tab that is open. The strip wraps rather than scrolling, so no tab is ever hidden past its right edge. The calls of the open tab run across the row as cells: `device.list` over `Every T3000 device…`, `device.describe` over `One device in full…`, as many per row as fit, each description cut to three lines with the whole sentence on hover, so one long sentence cannot set the height of every row. The name and its sentence share one face — a name in monospace read as code, and these are not code. The name is a **link** that selects that call in the runner, seeds its arguments and scrolls there, which is the only thing a reader clicking a name could have wanted. The open tab shows six cells and carries the way to the rest at the right end of the strip: `Show 2 more`, which names what is held back, and `Show less` once it is out. There is no reads-and-writes split; the calls that stop for a person are named in one line under the strip instead of being a heading. Fetched through `tools/list` when the page opens — which starts the server if it is not up — so the list cannot drift from what the model is offered.
- **Run one**: the picker the links drive, the arguments as JSON seeded from the chosen tool's schema, and the answer. It calls the same `callTool()` the loop uses, so a write still asks the person first: the page is a second way in to the same gate, not around it.

When the catalogue cannot be read, the page says why **and offers the Tool Server page** rather than an install button: the reason is almost always that nothing is running, and starting it is a decision that belongs where its state is shown.

`temcoAi.connection` is the other half:

- **Status**: a word with a dot beside it — `Running`, `Starting`, `Failed`, `Stopped` — then the address it answers on (`http://127.0.0.1:<port>/mcp`), how many tools it offers, its process id, and how long it has been up. Under them **Start**, **Stop**, **Restart**, and **Show the log**. The buttons disable themselves while a start is in flight and when they are the wrong verb for the state, so nothing offers "Stop" to something that is not running.
- **The program**: installed or not, where, the platform, and the artifact id — with the `dotnet publish` line when there is nothing installed, because a source checkout is the case that has no release to download from.

Both **Where it answers** and **Platform API** were removed from this page, and each for the same reason: a page about one program should describe that program, not ask the reader to configure something they cannot yet use.

| Removed | Why it was there | Where it lives now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Port field | A fixed port is only needed by something *outside* this window that has to reach a known address — an inspector, a script. Everyone else gets a free port per start, which is what stops two windows colliding | `temcoAi.serverPort`, in Settings. The address it produces is still shown, read-only, in **Status** |
| Platform API field | It names the HTTP service the device, repository and project tools ask. **No such service exists yet** (open decision 1), so the field asked a person to configure an address that could not answer | `temcoAi.platformApi`, in Settings; the `Temco AI: Platform API` command now opens the setting instead of a page |


Four things the page does not do, each a decision:

1. **Nothing starts on open.** A status page that changes what it reports is not a status page.
2. **Nothing is guessed.** A stop and a crash both end in a process that is gone; only the exit itself separates them, which is why the failure branch carries the last lines the process wrote.
3. **The log is a button, not a row.** It is a page about one program, and the log is one of the things that program produces.
4. **The state is repainted from the module, not re-rendered.** Start, stop and restart each answer with a `status` message that the page's own script paints into the four facts it owns, so a field halfway through being typed is never thrown away.

Three older dialogs were removed in favour of these pages: a status toast that said only that the server was ready, an input box that asked for an address without saying what it was for, and a row that opened a page with no status on it.
### 10. Security

| Concern | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| The model decides what to call | The loop is the boundary, not the model's goodwill: `DESTRUCTIVE` is a set in the extension, not a property read from the server |
| A write reaches equipment | Only after an explicit yes in the panel, and the yes is recorded with the arguments |
| The server is reachable from the network | It is not: the socket is bound to `127.0.0.1` by name, never `0.0.0.0` |
| Another process on this machine calls it | Every request but `/health` needs the bearer token this window generated at start. The token lives in memory, is passed in the child's environment rather than its arguments, and dies with the process |
| The catalog is modified | Opened `ReadOnly` (§5.6); writes stay in `temco-store` |
| A tool result is code | Results are text and are rendered as text, never evaluated |
| A downloaded server is tampered with | Pinned sha256 per platform where the catalogue has one; where it does not, the download is confirmed once with the file's origin and size in front of the person, and the checksum it had is recorded |
| Secrets | None are passed but the per-start token; the environment is a curated list, not a copy of the host's |

---

### 11. Decisions

Settled, with the reason each one was settled that way:

1. **The server is HTTP, and the window manages it.** `TEMCO_MCP_TRANSPORT=http` is what the application starts, on loopback, with a per-start token. The reason is not throughput: a pipe has no address and no state, so a page could only ever say "ready" or nothing. A program that writes needs a stop button, and a stop button needs a process someone owns. `stdio` stays as the second mode, because it is what an inspector and a script want, and it is one `switch` in `Program.cs`.
2. **The server is downloaded, not bundled.** 108 MB and 344 files per platform in every installer is worse than a verified download on first use through the machinery that already installs models. A release attaches `temco-mcp-<rid>.tar.gz` for each of its three platforms, and the catalogue asks for the one that matches the running application's version. Bundling stays available for an air-gapped build by adding the artifact to the catalogue with a `file://` URL.
3. **Two pages, not one.** The catalogue and the connection were one page for a while. They are two questions asked at different times, and the state in which the catalogue cannot be read is the state in which the connection is the only thing worth showing.
4. **The log is a button on the Tool Server page, not a row in the panel.** The panel names things a person goes to; a log is what a person goes to *after* something on a page has gone wrong.
5. **The supervisor starts the server, not the loop.** `runAgent` used to own the process because it was the only consumer. It has three now — the loop, the runner, the catalogue — and the page that shows the address has to be looking at the process the others use.
6. **A missing checksum is a question, not a refusal.** The entry used to demand a setting be found before the first download, which made "Start" a dead end on a machine that had never enabled it. The confirmation carries more information than the setting ever did — the exact URL, the size, the destination — and the observed checksum is recorded either way, so nothing is lost by asking instead of refusing. Pinning the hashes stays the goal; it is a data task that needs one build to exist first, and `allowUnpinned` now means only "do not ask".

Still open:

1. **Who answers `TEMCO_PLATFORM_API`.** The device, repo, and project tools need one HTTP surface. Either a service in the product answers it, or `IPlatformApi` gains an implementation that speaks T3000 protocols directly and the URL becomes a connection setting. Only `HttpPlatformApi` changes either way.
2. **Where the audit lives.** The server owns `audit.db` in this document, while the extension already owns the store schema. One has to be authoritative before the first release.
3. **Whether the 7B model may act by default.** The 1.5B model may not; the catalogue can carry a per-model `maxTools` and a `mayCallDestructive` flag.
4. **Whether one process should serve several windows.** The token is per window and the port is per window, so two windows run two processes. A shared process would need a shared token and a supervisor outside the extension host; nothing needs it yet.
5. **Whether `/health` should need the token.** It does not, so the supervisor can poll it before it knows anything. It exposes the name and version of a program whose binary is on the same disk.

---

### 12. Where the code is

| Section | File |
| --- | --- |
| 4, the runtime | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/runtime.ts` |
| 5.1 to 5.8, the server | `tools/temco-mcp/**` (both transports: `Program.cs`) |
| 6.1, 6.2, the client | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/mcp.ts` |
| 6.3, where the binary is | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/binPath.ts` |
| 6.4, the loop | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/agent.ts` |
| 6.5, the process, its port and its token | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/server.ts` |
| 7.2, the catalogue entries | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/catalog.ts` |
| 7.3, unpacking a tool server | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/install.ts` |
| 8.3, the environment | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/binPath.ts`, `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/server.ts` |
| 9, the groups and the two pages | `extensions/temco-ai/src/aiPages.ts`, `extensions/temco-ai/package.json` |
| 9, the commands | `extensions/temco-ai/src/extension.ts` |
| the one output channel | `extensions/temco-ai/src/ai/log.ts` |
| 7.4, the release step | `.github/workflows/release.yml` |

---

---
title: "Authoring parity with Dr.Explain, publishing parity with MSDN"
order: 110
edit: false
---

The product direction is two references at once, and they answer different questions:

- **Dr.Explain** is the *authoring* reference: how a person starts a project, brings existing material in, and edits topics with their properties in front of them.
- **MSDN** is the *output* reference: what a reader gets. The website is the product; CHM and PDF are the same tree in other clothes.

Where the two disagree, MSDN wins — a surface that looks like Dr.Explain but does not produce an MSDN-shaped site has failed the requirement.

## 1. Where we stand today

Everything below is what the code does, not what the mock draws.

| Thing | Where | Reality today |
|---|---|---|
| A project | catalog `documents` row (`extensions/temco-store/src/catalog/schema.ts`) | name, slug, version, audience, owner, timestamps |
| What it reads | `doc_sources` (many-to-many, per-source branch + include glob) | created by the wizard's **Sources** step |
| What it builds | `recipes` (`engine`, `config_path`, `script`, `outputs`) | the wizard writes `engine: 'docfx'` and the ticked outputs |
| Outputs offered | `extensions/temco-projects/src/projectFlow.ts` | website · PDF · offline help (CHM) · search index · AI index |
| Creation flow | `src/projectFlow.ts` | 4 steps: **Basics** (name, version, owner, audience) → **Sources** → **Outputs** → **Review** |
| Content | each product repository's `docs/help/registry.json` + Markdown (`extensions/temco-docs/src/registry.ts`) | the repository is the source of truth; the platform reads it |
| Page metadata already modelled | `RegistryPage` | `title, area, chapter, order, audience, ship, owner, tags, keywords, help_ids, code, reviewed` |
| Authoring already implemented | `newPagePage.ts` (3-step new page), `pageOps.ts` (`writePage` — the only disk write), `registryEdit.ts` (field writes) | a page is created from the location + template + draft, and registry fields survive regeneration |
| Builds | `extensions/temco-docs/src/builds.ts` | the **repository's own scripts** (`docs:site`, `docs:build`, `build:help:dry`) run as VS Code tasks, and success is judged by the artifacts |
| Checks | `diagnostics.ts`, `sections.ts` | structure, staleness, unreviewed pages |

Two consequences worth stating plainly:

1. **We already have the Dr.Explain page properties.** `ship` is its "export conditions", `help_ids` is its "help context ID", `keywords` is its index, `order` is its TOC position, and `owner`/`audience`/`reviewed` are its bookkeeping. Nothing in the data model is missing for authoring.
2. **We are missing the front door and the import.** Creation starts at "name, version, owner" — a project is assumed to have content already. There is no way to begin from a Word file, an HTML folder, a CHM, a template, or a repository.

## 2. Dr.Explain's flow, from the surfaces

Start page:

- four ways in — **Create a local project**, **Create a shared project on the server**, **Checkout project from the server**, **Open an existing project**;
- six importers — **Microsoft Word**, **RTF**, **Compiled HTML Help (CHM)**, **HLP**, **Standalone HTML**, **folder of HTML/TXT/XML**;
- **recent projects** and **templates in all languages**.

Editor:

- topic tree with a synthetic *Title* / *Table of contents* root and numbered topics;
- **properties panel** per topic: title, filename, export conditions, status, header/footer, page numbering, help context IDs;
- central page with **Menu / Index / Search** tabs;
- toolbar previews: **Content editor**, **HTML preview**, **CHM preview**, **PDF preview**;
- AI assistant rail with the model/provider setting.

## 3. Gap analysis

| Capability | Dr.Explain | Us today | What it means |
|---|---|---|---|
| Start from nothing | local project | **have it** (Basics step) | — |
| Start from a template | templates in all languages | no | a template is a repository-shaped skeleton; cheap and high value |
| Start from existing material | Word, RTF, CHM, HLP, HTML, folder | no | **the biggest gap** |
| Shared / checkout | server + checkout | git remote + clone exists in `temco-repositories`, not in the project flow | map to git, do not build a server |
| Topic tree editing | drag, renumber, nest | tree view + `order` field | add reorder → `order` write |
| Per-topic properties | full panel | registry fields, but no form | **form over `registryEdit`** |
| Export conditions | per topic | `ship` | expose it |
| Context IDs / index | per topic | `help_ids`, `keywords` | expose them |
| WYSIWYG body editing | HTML editor inside the tool | Markdown in the editor + rendered preview | do **not** copy; MSDN topics are Markdown |
| Preview before build | HTML · CHM · PDF | HTML preview exists; CHM/PDF only after a build | add CHM/PDF preview after first build |
| MSDN-shaped site | — | the site is the repository generator's; our output choice is `website` | make the contract explicit (§5) |

## 4. Decisions

**D1 — Creation asks *where the content comes from*, not a cosmetic "type".**
The origin decides which steps follow and what gets written on *Create*. A dropdown that changes nothing is worse than none.

| Origin | What it does on Create | Writes |
|---|---|---|
| **Blank project** | creates the project; the first page is authored here | `documents` + `recipes` |
| **From template** | copies a template folder into the chosen location, then the project cites it | template tree + `documents` + `doc_sources` |
| **From a repository** (Dr.Explain's "shared") | clones/attaches a remote at a branch and reads its `docs/help/registry.json` | `sources` + `doc_sources` |
| **Import a document set** | converts the material into pages, then the project cites the folder | Markdown tree + registry entries + `doc_sources` |
| **Open existing** | picks a folder that already holds a registry | `sources` only |

**D2 — Importers write the repository's contract, never a second content store.**
An importer is a converter: source → `docs/help/*.md` + `docs/help/registry.json`, exactly the shape the product's own generator writes. The platform database stays what it is (a register of projects, sources, builds, releases) — otherwise the same text would live twice and drift.

**D3 — MSDN is an output contract, not a theme.** See §5.

**D4 — Page properties are ours already; add only what MSDN needs.**
Expose `ship`, `order`, `keywords`, `help_ids`, `audience`, `owner` through a form in the page editor. Two new optional fields are worth having: `kind` (concept · how-to · reference · tutorial — MSDN's `ms.topic`) and `applies_to` (the product versions a page is for). Header/footer and page numbering are **template** settings for PDF, not per page.

**D5 — Collaboration is git, not a server.** "Shared project" = a project whose source is a repository with a remote; "checkout" = clone + branch; review is a pull request. `temco-repositories` already clones, pulls and records branches. Building a Dr.Explain server would duplicate what the repository host already does better.

**D6 — The website is the product; CHM and PDF derive from it.**
One tree, three targets: website (primary), CHM (same topics, `help_ids` → HH context IDs, `keywords` → index), PDF (one print profile).

## 5. The MSDN contract (what "similar to MSDN" means concretely)

**Structure.** `area → chapter → topic`, the shape the registry already has. A topic is one Markdown file; `order` decides TOC position; a page not in the registry is not published.

**URL.** `/{area}/{chapter}/{slug}` — stable, lowercase, no build hash. Renaming a slug writes a redirect entry, because links outlive builds.

**Every topic page carries:**

- breadcrumb `Area / Chapter / Title`;
- left **TOC** of the whole set with the current branch open;
- an **on-page TOC** built from `##`/`###`;
- **Applies to** (from `applies_to`, or the project version);
- **previous / next** in TOC order;
- **feedback** and a "this page is stale" signal from `reviewed`;
- a permalink for every heading.

**Index and search.** `keywords` feeds the site index; the search index (already a wizard target) is built from titles, headings and keywords, so a reader can reach a page by a controller's name as well as by its title.

**Version selector.** A released version is a `releases` row; the site serves the newest plus archived versions. The version selector is a list of those rows, not a rebuild.

**CHM.** The same tree compiled with `help_ids` as context IDs, `keywords` as the index, and the chapters as the TOC levels. A topic that is `ship: false` is in the site's draft preview and not in the CHM.

**Where the furniture is produced — and why Temco Learn is not it.** A topic page is rendered by the product repository's own site generator (`docs:site`), and Learn's *Article Preview* opens the published file rather than drawing a second copy of it (`extensions/temco-workbench/src/learnPages.ts`: *“a preview that can disagree with the shipped page is worse than no preview”*). So the platform's half of the contract is **the metadata that makes the furniture possible** — `order` for the TOC, `keywords` for the index, `ship` for what is published, `help_ids` for the CHM, `title` for the heading and the breadcrumb — and the generator's half is drawing it. Adding a page-properties form (phase 2, below) is what closes the loop between them.

## 6. The project flow, redesigned

Five steps; step 2–4 are the steps we have, unchanged in content.

1. **Origin** — Blank · Template · Repository · Import · Open existing.
2. **Basics** — name, version, owner, audience (today's step 1).
3. **Bring in** — *shape follows the origin*: template picker · repository picker (remote + branch) · importer picker (Word/RTF/HTML/HTML folder/CHM) with a destination folder · folder picker.
4. **What it builds** — the targets we already offer, with **Website** ticked for a new project.
5. **Review** — what will be written, and where the files land. Nothing is written before this step.

Two rules stay: nothing is written until the last button, and the project you create becomes the selected project.

## 7. Phases

| Phase | Deliverable | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | `documents.origin` + `documents.template` (schema 4, with a migration); wizard step **Origin** (blank · repository · folder · template · import), where the origin decides what the third step asks; a template writes a starter `docs/help` tree and refuses to overwrite anything; folder and template projects record their folder as a source | **done** |
| **2** | Page-properties form (`temcoDocs.pageProperties`, Markdown title-bar action) over `registryEdit`: title, owner, audience, order, ships, keywords, tags, `help_ids`, with the confirmation naming every value it will write | **done** |
| **3** | Importers (`temcoDocs.import`, called by the wizard's *Import a document set* origin): **HTML folder / page** and **Markdown folder** natively; **Word / RTF / ODT** through pandoc; **CHM** through `hh.exe -decompile` (Windows). All four write the repository contract and refuse a folder that already has a registry | **done** |
| **4** | The platform's half of the MSDN contract: the metadata itself (`title`, `order`, `keywords`, `ship`, `help_ids`, `audience`), which phase 2 made editable and which the module's existing validation already reports on. A separate readiness page was built and then removed: it re-tested the same fields without adding a decision, and the Pipeline group already has Review Center and Build Center. The drawing half — breadcrumb, on-page TOC, prev/next, permalinks, redirects, version selector — belongs to each product repository's `docs:site` generator, which is not in this repository | **platform half done** |
| **5** | CHM/PDF previews after a build (`temcoDocs.openArtifact`, from the Build Center's artifact rows); drag reorder → `order` (already in `pageOps.handleDrop`); the repository origin cites recorded sources and links to the Sources page, where `temcoRepositories.fetchSource` clones | **done** |

**Phase 1 is the answer to "do we add more types?"** — yes, five origins, and each one changes what the next step asks.

## 8. What we deliberately do not copy

- **Its WYSIWYG HTML editor.** MSDN topics are Markdown; the editor is VS Code's, and the page renders beside it.
- **Its project database.** The repository keeps the text; the platform keeps the register.
- **Its server and checkout.** Git and pull requests.
- **Its single-file exports as the main path.** The site is the product; CHM is one of its outputs.


---

---
title: "Modules"
order: 200
edit: false
---

A module is a real VS Code extension inside the application. Namespaces follow the module ([../architecture/naming.md](../naming)).

## Register

| Module | Purpose | Namespace | State |
|---|---|---|---|
| `temco-workbench` | the application's own surfaces: the Library, the dashboard, what needs doing | `temcoWorkbench` | **active** |
| `temco-ui` | where a page of ours opens, what a command argument means, and the shared page components (hero, figures, sections, cards) | `temcoUi` | **active** |
| `temco-store` | the platform's own memory: the catalog database, the selection, the naming rules | `temcoStore` | **active** |
| `temco-docs` | the registry, pages, checks, and the writes | `temcoDocs` | **active** |
| `temco-learn` | the published site: its app, the site folder, the panel, the preview, the downloads the deploy cannot make, and the push to `gh-pages` | `temcoLearn` | **active** |
| `temco-editor` | the editor for a page: the Markdown surface, its ribbon, its engine seam | `temcoEditor` | **active** |
| `temco-repositories` | the organisation, local copies, the working area, reading a source | `temcoRepositories` | **active** |
| `temco-ai` | the local runtime and models, the prompts, the panel, drafts and answers | `temcoAi` | **active** |
| `temco-projects` | projects, sources, outputs, builds | `temcoProjects` | **active** |
| `temco-sql` | registers, queries, export | `temcoSql` | reserved |
| `temco-nettools` | devices on the wire | `temcoNettools` | reserved |
| `temco-bacnet` | objects, points, trends | `temcoBacnet` | reserved |
| `temco-device-manager` | fleets, firmware, health | `temcoDevices` | reserved |
| `temco-tools` | the Tools container until those four modules exist: Devices, I/O Studio, BACnet Studio, Network and SQL Studio, as pages over a sample | `temcoTools` | **active** |

## States

| State | Contents | Build effect |
|---|---|---|
| **reserved** | `README.md` only | the build ignores the folder: nothing is compiled, nothing ships, no container appears |
| **active** | `package.json`, `tsconfig.json`, `src/` | compiled and shipped as a built-in extension |

A module becomes active on the day it has a first real feature. Reserving a module with a manifest would ship an extension with nothing in it, which is why a folder becomes a module only when it has something to do.

## Ownership

**`temco-store`** — the platform's own memory, and only that: one SQLite file per window (sources read, projects, their sources, the pages a read found, builds, artifacts, commits, events), the **selection** (which project the window is on, and the page it last opened), and the naming rules the tables are keyed by (a slug). It has one command, no views and no settings. It is the base every other module stands on, and it is deliberately *not* `temco-sql`: that module is about the databases a **product** uses, and it neither owns nor knows about this file.

**`temco-docs`** — the content side: the registry of areas, chapters and pages, the structural checks, the **Workspace tree** (its own view), the pages, the Markdown surfaces, the reports and the builds, and the five writes. It owns the only write a page ever gets, and it publishes the documentation model other modules read (`src/api.ts`) — the Library and the dashboard are the workbench's, and they read that model rather than a copy of it.

**`temco-ui`** — the shared UI conventions, and nothing else: the editor column a page of ours opens in (one group, never a split), what a command argument names (a slug, a row, or a row's own command argument), and the page components a first screen is built from — the banded hero, the band of figures, a section heading, and a row of cards. It has no command, no view and no setting.

**`temco-workbench`** — the application's own surfaces, and the only module that is not a domain: the **Library** (the index of what every module has; each row's action is that module's own command), the **dashboard** (the application talking about itself), the **needs page**, which tab opens at startup, and which sidebar section comes forward when the project changes. It writes nothing and owns no data: its rows come from the store and from the documentation module's published model.

**`temco-ai`** — the local model: the runtime and models downloaded on request into the user's own folder, the prompts, the AI panel, drafts and their diffs, and the citations that open the page they name. It reads the documentation through `temco-docs` and never writes a page.

### How two modules may talk

Only one shape is allowed, and it runs in one direction:

* **A provider publishes, a consumer declares.** `temco-ai` requires `temcocontrols.temco-docs` (`extensionDependencies`), so that module activates first and its exports — the sets, the pages, the selection, the editor column, one change signal — are there when the consumer's `activate()` runs. The shapes are spelled twice, once in each module (`src/api.ts` in the provider, `src/docs.ts` in the consumer), because an extension cannot import another extension's sources.
* **A consumer never requires a provider that requires it.** That is a cycle, and a cycle is a load-order bug waiting to happen. The second direction is always a *command* the first module calls and may find missing, and the caller gates what it shows on a context key it sets itself (`temcoDocs.aiInstalled`).

`temco-docs` and `temco-ai` are the worked example: one declared dependency, one optional command surface. `temco-store` is the other shape a module can have — a base with no surface of its own, which every other module requires and none calls back into.

`npm run check-temco` refuses the mistakes this section warns about: a dependency graph with a circle in it (VS Code disables *every* extension in a circle, so the application comes up with a silent sidebar), a menu, submenu or welcome link that names an id no module declares, a view contributed under a container no module declares (the workbench shows nothing at all — no icon, no panel, no error), a container id two modules both claim, and a module-level binding that collides with the CommonJS wrapper (`module`, `require`, …), which throws before the extension runs.

**`temco-repositories`** — the organisation and the local copies: the repository list, a source's files, cloning one branch with full history, `pull --ff-only`, removing the local copy, reading a file's text, **reading a source** — the registry a repository declares, from a folder or over the API, recorded into the store — and the **vocabulary of the repository's own documentation scripts** (which scripts exist and what each is for). It owns the working area (`temcoRepositories.workspaceFolder`, default `~/Temco`). It reads the platform's memory through `temco-store` and the shared page rules through `temco-ui`, and it needs nothing of `temco-docs`: what a registry *means* is that module's business, what a repository *has* is this one's.

**`temco-projects`** — a project as a first-class thing: name, version, audience, owner, the sources it is made of, the outputs it produces, and what building one left behind. Its pages are the list, the hub and the edit form. It reads sources and the repository's own scripts through `temco-repositories`, the platform's memory through `temco-store`, and it needs nothing from `temco-docs`: the pages a project *has* were recorded in the store by a read.

**`temco-sql`** — registers, queries and export against the databases the products use. It owns its own connections and storage.

**`temco-nettools`** — discovery, addressing, capture and inspection. The transport layer the device modules build on.

**`temco-bacnet`** — BACnet objects, points and trends, read through `temco-nettools` transports. It owns its object and point models and its storage.

**`temco-device-manager`** — fleets and health: inventory, firmware, status and the actions a person takes on a device. It reads devices through `temco-bacnet` and `temco-nettools`.

## Folder

```text
extensions/temco-<module>/
├── README.md              purpose, identifiers, what it owns — the only file a reserved module has
├── package.json           id, engine range, commands, views, settings, activation
├── tsconfig.json          extends ../tsconfig.base.json
├── src/
│   ├── extension.ts       the only activation entry: register, wire, dispose
│   └── <feature>/
├── media/                 icons, webview css and js
├── prompts/               model instructions as text, where a module needs them
└── scripts/               module-local gates
```

Its documentation is `docs/temco-platform/modules/temco-<module>/` **in this repository**, not a `docs` folder inside the module.
There is no `.vscodeignore`: the platform build packages a built-in module, `vsce` does not.

## Contract

1. Modules talk through commands, settings, files and events. No module imports another module's source.
2. The catalog belongs to `temco-docs`. Other modules read it through commands.
3. Each module owns its own storage under its own extension id — never inside a repository, never committed.
4. One module, one namespace. A command id is registered exactly once.
5. A module is a built-in extension. It uses the published `vscode` API only and never the shell's internals, so it still runs in an ordinary VS Code window.
6. A module never assumes the shell.

## Adding a module

1. Create the folder with the shape above.
2. Write `package.json` with its id, commands, views, settings and activation events.
3. `tsconfig.json` extends `../tsconfig.base.json`.
4. Store everything under its own id's storage.
5. Add its gate to the release list: `tsc`, plus its own behaviour check where it has one.
6. Write its page in `docs/temco-platform/modules/temco-<module>/`: commands, settings, storage, and what it must not own.
7. Compile it with the platform build (`npm run gulp compile-extension:temco-<module>`) and check it loads.

Nothing else is required: the build picks up any folder with a `package.json`, the root install brings in its dependencies, and web builds skip it automatically.


---

---
title: "`temco-ai`"
order: 210
edit: false
---

The local-AI module: it runs a model on this machine and answers from the documentation it is given.

| | |
|---|---|
| Folder | `extensions/temco-ai` |
| Extension id | `temcocontrols.temco-ai` |
| Namespace | `temcoAi` |
| State | active |
| Container | **Temco AI** (`temcoAi`) in the right side bar — the panel (`temcoAi.panel`) |
| Requires | `temcocontrols.temco-docs` |
| Settings | `temcoAi.modelFolder`, `temcoAi.allowUnpinned`, `temcoAi.runtime`, `temcoAi.model`, `temcoAi.idleMinutes` |

## Responsibilities, one file each

| Module | Owns |
|---|---|
| `src/extension.ts` | activation, the panel, and the model the rest of the platform reads (`status`, `facts`, `modelLabel`, `analyse`, `draft`) |
| `src/docs.ts` | what this module reads of `temco-docs` — the contract in one file, and the only place that crosses modules |
| `src/surface.ts` | the AI page's view type |
| `src/aiPage.ts` | the page: the runtime, the model, the folder they live in, the downloads, and the log |
| `src/ai/catalog.ts` | what can be downloaded: the pinned llama.cpp build with the checksums GitHub publishes, the models on offer, their licences. No network, no filesystem |
| `src/ai/install.ts` | the model folder, the resumable download and its checksum check, the unpack, and the manifest of what is installed |
| `src/ai/runtime.ts` | the local server process: started on demand on a loopback port, health-checked, stopped when idle — and the single `complete()` call everything else uses |
| `src/ai/machine.ts` | what this machine can run, and which catalog entry suits it |
| `src/ai/history.ts` | the commits a draft is about: `git log` over the page's `code` globs since its last review, metadata and paths only. Never throws |
| `src/ai/prompts.ts` | loads `prompts/*.md` and fills them in: the house rules, the question, the draft, the area, a new page. The only text that may reach the model |
| `src/ai/citations.ts` | what an answer pointed at: the ids that resolve, the ids that do not, and the plain term search behind a low-confidence fallback |
| `src/ai/panel.ts` | the panel: chat about the open page, `Draft update` as a diff, `Ask the area`, and the model line. Decides nothing itself |
| `src/ai/commands.ts` | the list a person picks from: install, use, remove, open the folder, and the AI's status |
| `media/` | the panel's script and styles (`ai-panel.js` is a browser asset, not extension code) and the module's icon |
| `prompts/` | the templates the module sends to the model, one file per task |

## Commands

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `temcoAi.page` | opens the AI page, or one of its sections (`downloads`) |
| `temcoAi.openPanel` | focuses the panel in the right side bar |
| `temcoAi.models` | the list of runtimes and models: what is installed, what can be downloaded, which one is in use |
| `temcoAi.removeModel` | deletes an installed runtime or model, and clears it from the settings when it was the one in use |
| `temcoAi.openModelFolder` | opens the folder the downloads live in |
| `temcoAi.dockRight` | shows the AI in the right side bar |
| `temcoAi.status` | which runtime and model are in use, whether the server is running, and how to stop it or read its log |

## How it reads the documentation

One declared dependency, in one direction:

- this module **requires** `temcocontrols.temco-docs`, so that module is activated first and its exports — the
  sets, `collectPages`, `pageForUri`, the selection, the editor column, one change signal — are ready when this
  module's `activate()` runs. `src/docs.ts` is where they are declared and where a call reaches them.
- the documentation module **requires nothing** of this one. It calls `temcoAi.page` and `temcoAi.models` for what
  a person opens, and asks this module's exports for the two things that are work rather than navigation: a body
  to draft, and a source to analyse. It gates the rows and menu entries it shows on `temcoDocs.aiInstalled`, so an
  install without this module shows no door that does not open.

## The tool server

The assistant can call tools: a C# MCP server lives at `tools/temco-mcp` and this module is its client. The protocol between them, the tools it exposes, how the server is built, and how the application starts and stops it are specified in [`design/ai-assistant-and-mcp-server.md`](../../design/ai-assistant-and-mcp-server).

## What it never does

- Writes a page. A draft goes back to the page that asked for it, and a person accepts it as one undoable edit.
- Leaves the machine. The runtime listens on loopback, models arrive only from a command a person ran, and the
  only thing that ever leaves is an API read the documentation module asked for.
- Ships native code. A compiled engine is per-OS, so it is downloaded on request into the user's own folder.


---

---
title: "Temco Docs — documentation"
order: 220
edit: false
---

This folder documents the **Temco Docs module** (`extensions/temco-docs`): what it does, how it is built, what it
assumes, and where it is going. The platform-wide design is [design/temco-learn/](https://github.com/temcocontrols/temco-platform/blob/main/docs/temco-learn/design/README.md).

The product documentation itself is not here: the pages and the registry live in the product repositories,
because that is where the content has to be.

| Document | Read it when |
|---|---|
| [`architecture.md`](architecture) | you need the whole picture: the repositories, the flow, how it ships |
| [`registry-schema.md`](registry-schema) | you touch anything that reads or writes registry data |
| [`extension-design.md`](extension-design) | you add a view, a command, a check or a diagnostic |
| [`platform-design.md`](../../ui/platform-design) | you work on the platform itself: sources, the documents a person creates, builds and artifacts, the AI over history, and the catalog |
| [`editor-design.md`](../../ui/editor-design) | you work on editing: the toolbar, creating a page, images, drafts, and the write rule they wait on |
| [`app-flow.md`](../../ui/app-flow) | you work on the left toolbar, a panel, or where a row leads: the four containers, what each view asks, and the page behind every row |
| [`authoring-flow.md`](../../ui/authoring-flow) | you work on screenshots, the side bar, or when a page must be re-illustrated: the capture stage, the two views, and what still waits on a decision |
| [`app-layout.md`](../../ui/app-layout) | you need the target UI: the application window, the site's generated dashboard, and the seam between them |
| [`design/temco-learn/`](https://github.com/temcocontrols/temco-platform/blob/main/docs/temco-learn/design/README.md) | you need the platform design itself: content model, registry, search, the offline CHM, editing, feedback, AI, governance |

## The three names, and why they are different

| Name | What it is |
|---|---|
| **Temco Platform** | the application: the branded build of VS Code, with these modules built in |
| **Temco Docs** | this module — the documentation features, in the application and in an ordinary VS Code window |
| **Temco Learn** | the published site: the documentation people read |

One name for the tool, one for the module, one for the artifact. The module maintains content; Temco Learn
displays it. Keeping them apart is what stops "put it in Temco Learn" from being ambiguous.

## The guide, and why a module has one

`temcoDocs.overview` opens **Documentation** (`src/overview.ts`): the tree answers "where is this page", and this page answers "what am I looking at" — pages, areas, how much is behind its source, which faults the checks found, and the three checks that can be run from here. Every number is computed from the same registry the tree reads, so the page and the tree cannot disagree. It is also the **one owner of per-area coverage**: a *Coverage & Health* page used to exist on the workbench dashboard, drawn from this same registry, and was removed rather than left to drift.

**How To Use** was removed: it was a mock guide whose numbers were typed in (“13.0 is current”, “17 pages are waiting”), and a guide that quotes stale figures teaches the wrong thing.

The rest of the container is the mock's Documentation module, page for page: **Projects**, a repository, **Sources**, **Documents** and a page, a **Source Explorer**, **Review Center**, **Build Center** and **Releases** (`src/docsPages.ts`). The side bar holds the mock's sections and rows — Pages, Projects, Sources, Areas, Pipeline, Working Set — plus the page tree itself, which is why there is no separate *Docs Explorer* activity-bar icon: one icon browses one object.

**The Review Center** is a queue and the draft it opens: a vertical tab list on the left (each row the draft's name, the id it proposes, how long it has waited, and the source it read), and on the right exactly one draft in full — its kind, its line count, the text exactly as it will be written, and the two acts that settle it. Accept and Discard live in the draft rather than on the queue's rows, because an act that writes a page belongs beside the text it writes and a button on a row invites settling a draft nobody has read; the queue's rows are a tab list, so a keyboard reaches them the way it reaches the tabs on Publish & Releases. Only the newest is marked `Latest`, and choosing another row is what makes it the one on the right.

**The Build Center** is two answers, in the order a person needs them. **What this project builds** — a card per output the project was created with (Website, PDF, Offline help, Search index, AI index), drawn in a row like the create form's own ticks: what the output is for, the script it would run *here*, the files it has now, and the sentence saying why when this repository has no script for it. Only a card with a script carries a **Build** button, and the card itself is never the button — reading a target must not start a build, and a target that cannot be made is reported rather than approximated with a different one. **Builds** — the runs, newest first, as a record rather than as a stack of cards: one list, a hairline between two runs, `#12`, `Latest`, when it started, how long it took counted from the two timestamps, and the verdict as a word. It folds after five runs. Above them, four figures state the project at a glance (builds · files · written · last build), and the files themselves are not reprinted here: they are listed on **Publish & Releases**, one block per output, with the fold a hundred and twenty pages need. A build history is a sequence, and a box around every run says each one is an unrelated object.

The title block carries **only the two acts this page owns** — `Build the Site` and `Build the Offline Help` — plus the per-output `Build` buttons on the rows below them (`temcoDocs.buildOutput`). `Refresh` was removed (reopening a page re-reads it) and `Publish & Releases` became a link in the files section it belongs to: three of four buttons in a title block were navigation, which is what made the page read as a menu rather than a record.

Every one of them is drawn with the shared page vocabulary from `temco-ui` (`page.css` + `page()`), so they are the pages whose look is the mock's design rather than a private `<style>` block. The pattern they establish is the one the module's other pages move to: a page describes what it draws, and `temco-ui` decides how that looks and how a click becomes a command.

**Publish & Releases** reads the same list of outputs, in the same order, and shows them as the tabs of "The files a reader can be given": a row of tabs (built, with its file count and size; or the line saying what makes it, since the search index is written by the site when a build is imported; or dashed and greyed when the create form did not tick it), and under the row exactly one panel — the files of the chosen output, the first six and a native **Show N more files** / **Show less** fold, or the callout saying why there is nothing to list. Choosing a tab is presentation only, answered by a small script the page carries: every panel is rendered, the first is open, and a window with scripts off still shows the files. The tabs are a real tab list — `aria-selected`, a roving `tabindex`, Arrow/Home/End — and the categories are never derived from whatever happens to be in the artifacts table: a target a person asked for is shown whether or not a build has produced it yet.

## Where the platform design lives

[`temco-learn/design/`](https://github.com/temcocontrols/temco-platform/blob/main/docs/temco-learn/design/README.md) — 17 documents covering the content model, the
registry, search, the offline CHM, the editing and git flow, feedback, the AI features, the implementation
plan and the governance rules. `16-overview.md` is the one-page version.

[`design/authoring-and-msdn-output.md`](../../design/authoring-and-msdn-output) is the other half: what an
author gets, and what a reader gets. Its §5 is the **MSDN contract** — the concrete meaning of "looks like
Microsoft Learn" that a surface is judged against — and §6 is the project flow that follows from it.

**They describe the platform, and their paths belong to the product repository.** `docs/t3000/**`,
`docs/help/registry.json`, `docs/docfx.json`, `scripts/docs-*.mjs` and `dist/docs/` are the *product* repo's
paths, not this one's: the content and the publishing pipeline stay there by design.

Where the two disagree, the design package wins — and the disagreement is a bug worth fixing in one place or
the other.


---

---
title: "Architecture"
order: 221
edit: false
---

## The repositories

```
C:\QN\temcocontrols\
│
├─ T3000Webview-EEZ-Studio        the product repo — the content lives here
│   ├─ docs/t3000/**              the pages, one folder per chapter
│   ├─ docs/help/registry.json    the structure: areas, pages, owners, order
│   ├─ docs/help/index.json       generated: headings + git provenance
│   ├─ docs/toc.yml  **/toc.yml   generated navigation
│   ├─ docs/templates/t3000/      the site template: brand, page-meta bar
│   ├─ dist/docs/                 the rendered copy, and the package the site reads
│   └─ scripts/docs-*.mjs         the build side: registry, gates, changelog, media
│
├─ temco-platform                 this repository — the fork of VS Code: the shell, the modules, the build
│   ├─ extensions/temco-docs/     the module — every documentation feature lives here
│   │   ├─ src/registry.ts        reads the registry, runs the structural checks
│   │   ├─ src/tree.ts            the Content view: areas, chapters, pages, gaps
│   │   ├─ src/diagnostics.ts     problems, in the file that has to be fixed
│   │   ├─ src/extension.ts       view, status bar, commands, file watcher
│   │   └─ src/…                  builds, report, scaffold, feedback, ai/ — the module table is in extension-design.md
│   │   └─ media/docs.svg         the view icon
│   ├─ docs/temco-learn/design/ the platform design (17 documents)
│   └─ product.json               branding of the application: name, icon, protocol, gallery
│
└─ docfx                          the publishing engine — upstream, pinned
                                  never modified; a version bump only
```

## The flow

```
   open a product repo in the editor
        │
        ▼
   read     docs/help/registry.json   →  areas · chapters · pages · owners · order
        │
        ▼
   show     Content view              →  pages, chapters, issues, never reviewed
        │                                Gaps → only the pages with a real fault
        ▼
   check    file exists · id agrees with area/chapter · title unique · order unique
        │
        ▼
   fetch    the runtime and a model are downloaded on request, into the user's own folder
        │
        ▼
   serve    the local server starts on demand, on loopback only, and stops when it is idle
        │
        ▼
   draft    the local AI reads a commit range and proposes page edits
        │
        ▼
   approve  a named human accepts; nothing else ever writes
        │
        ▼
   publish  the engine         →  the package  →  the site
            the help build     →  CHM          →  ships with the installer
```

## How it ships

```
   the application   →  TemcoStudio-Setup.exe
                        ├─ the branded build of VS Code  ("Temco Platform")
                        └─ + the module, built in — one installer, one update path

   plain VS Code     →  the same module, run from the platform's sources
                        (it uses the published `vscode` API only — nothing shell-specific)

   same module · same views · same checks · same AI — the only difference is who builds it

   what a customer installation gets

   T3000
     └─ T3000_Help.chm      the only artifact from this platform
                            no extension, no editor, no AI, no registry
```

## What it publishes (target)

```
   /                          the home page: what is inside, what changed recently
   /t3000/latest/…            a product, current — the pages exactly as they are today
   /t3000/13.0/…              the same pages for a pinned version
   /t3000/13.0/downloads      the PDF, the help file and the offline copy
   /_admin/                   health: owners, gaps, staleness — noindex
   /firmware/                 a second product later, from the same pipeline
```

## The three boundaries that must not blur

1. **Content never moves.** Pages stay at `docs/t3000/**` in the product repo, which is why the application,
   the MCP tools and the F1 help keep working untouched. This extension reads them, and writes to one only when
   a maintainer asks and confirms the exact path: the file for a page the registry declares, a draft they
   applied, or an empty registry where a folder they have open has none. Nothing under `_admin/` or `_edit/` is
   ever written — those are generated pages — and nothing else in the folder is touched. Nothing is ever
   committed. One deliberate
   exception: the platform **design** lives at `docs/temco-learn/design/`, because the design of a thing belongs
   with the thing. The product repo keeps the content and the pipeline; it does not register or publish the design
   pages.
2. **The application is packaging, not features.** The shell holds the branding, the default layout and the build.
   A module must never depend on something only the shell provides, or it stops working in plain VS Code.
3. **One artifact crosses into a customer installation.** The CHM. No editor, no registry, no AI runtime.

## Decisions already made, so they are not re-litigated

- The publishing engine is **pinned, never patched** unless a real need appears; the fork of it, if it ever
  happens, lives as commits in the project's own repo, not as loose patch files.
- One repository for the whole product. No submodules in either direction — the module is built in, not
  consumed as a package.
- The AI drafts and a named human approves. Nothing publishes itself.


---

---
title: "Extension design"
order: 222
edit: false
---

> **Layout superseded.** The views, the Pages tree behaviour and the flow are defined in [../../ui/redesign.md](../../ui/redesign). Files such as `home.ts` and `views.ts` mentioned below do not exist; the code is under `extensions/temco-docs/src/`.

### Responsibilities, one file each

| Module | Owns |
|---|---|
| `src/registry.ts` | reading `docs/help/registry.json` and the generated `index.json`/`page-meta.json`, the page id → file rule, the structural checks, the counts. No VS Code UI beyond `Uri`, `WorkspaceFolder` and the filesystem |
