owns the button (`say` / `complain` in `src/extension.ts`), which is why the area has two tabs: `Output` is this window's
command lines, `Publish` is the publish page's log, which a person used to have to leave the page to read.

Every line is cleaned before it is drawn (`cleanLine`): ANSI colour codes stripped (an escape sequence arrives as `ΓÇö` in a
console that is not a terminal), control characters dropped, the module's own `[site]` / `[git]` prefixes and the `$` prompt
removed, and the glyphs a build or a dev server uses to decorate its output (`➜`, `✓`, box drawing) taken out — the words
they decorate are kept. The raw lines stay in the output channel, where a terminal is the right place for them.

**One port, one way of serving.** `serve` listens on 1025 (`temcoLearn.sitePort`, above 1024 so it binds without root on macOS
and Linux) and shows `dist/` as it was last built. There is no second mode: `client-dev` — Vite with hot reload — exists for
editing the site's own React and Markdown, which is the repository's business and not something this page decides, so a
terminal still runs it (`npm run client-dev -- --port 1025`). A page that serves what was built has one way to serve it, and a
switch that changes nothing for the reader is a question nobody asked. A change to the port takes effect the next time the
server starts, and the log says so.

**Publishing is one page and one press** (`src/publishPage.ts`, `temcoLearn.publishing`). The row reads `Publish the Site` —
the count is already at its right-hand end as a badge, so the label says what is published and the number stays a number. The
row opens the page rather than acting on the click, and the page has four sections, each with the 2 px brand bar:

- **The repository** — one card, not a band of four cells. A head line of **links** (the remote opens the repo on GitHub, the
  commit opens that commit, the branch sits plain between them), then `12 paths in this commit` and the first **five** of them,
  each a link to the file on GitHub. The rest fold into a native `<details>` — `Show 7 more paths` — so a publish that touches
  forty files reads as six lines and expands in place, with no script and no round-trip. The foot says whether anything is
  unpushed and offers `Open the clone` · `Terminal`.
- **What publishing does** — the four step cards, each ending in the act that performs it.
- **Where it goes** — one card for the one subject: `ADDRESS` as the page's biggest **link** (it opens the live site), then
  `Served from` (main, built by GitHub Actions, handed to Pages), `Last publish` and `Waiting` as label/value pairs.
- **The last attempt** — the lines git printed, unchanged.

The button that does it (`temcoLearn.publishNow`) writes its result where the decision was made, so there is no preview strip
to read it in and no second page to be redirected to.

**Artifacts has a page** (`src/downloadPage.ts`, `temcoLearn.downloads`), opened by the Artifacts view's empty row and by its
title bar. Three sections, each with the 2 px brand bar the other pages carry: **What a build writes** (five cards — the
website, the help file, the pdf, the archive, the Word document), **Where it lands** (one card, because there is one working
area: the folder written out as the card's own **link**, and two lines spelling out `<project>/dist` and the `HelpDocs`
folder), and **The files on this machine** (a grid of **file cards** per project, packed **from the left** at the width the
content asks for — `minmax(215px, 240px)` with `justify-content: start`, because letting them stretch put one file in a card
900px wide — each with the glyph for its kind, the name, `offline help · 1.1 MB`, and the two acts a file on disk has:
`Show in the folder` for every card, and `Save a copy` where a copy can be taken). `Look` used to sit there and drew the
viewer into the inventory; looking at a `.pdf` or the built site is the panel's job and the OS's, so the card finds the file
instead and gets out of the way.

The page lists only what a build produces **as a whole**. A loose `.html` in `dist` is a page someone was iterating on, not an
output: listing it beside `T3000.pdf` made the page read like a folder dump, so it is filtered out — and a project whose only
result is such a fragment is not a project here at all, which is why the count in the title block counts the cards rather than
the files on disk. The panel's Artifacts view draws the same line: it lists what can be taken away, never a fragment.
**Publish** closes that view as `Publish the Site` — the count sits at the row's right-hand end as a badge, and the tooltip
carries the last publish. Publishing is about every build at once, which is why it is the one act that sits with them.

Nothing is invented: a project with no `.chm` has no `.chm` row, and the panel says `Nothing built on this machine yet` only
when nothing exists at all. Publishing state is read from git
(`src/state.ts`) in the clone, so two machines never disagree about what is out.

Answers appear in the **preview's own strip**, under its bar, and never in a notification: adding a build, building,
publishing, and every failure say what happened there, with at most one thing to do next. Only the *inputs* stay native — the
version box, and the build picker when a row did not name its build, and the folder dialog for a clone that has not been
chosen yet.

**Settings → General is a bundled Fluent page** (`webview/settings/`, React 19 + Fluent UI v9), not markup generated in the
extension host: the tokens are Fluent's, the brand is Azure's blue, and every control is a real Fluent control. It is drawn
in the house page language rather than a settings template — the brand wash with an eyebrow, a title and a note; one tinted
callout; a section heading with the 2px Azure rule before it; an entry row per setting with its control at the right-hand
end; a facts band; and links at the foot of the last block. `main.tsx` imports `temco-ui`'s `media/page.css`, so the wash, the
rule, the row and the buttons are the same rules Temco Learn's Overview page is drawn by and the two pages cannot drift.

The host's half is narrow: `src/settingsPages.ts` builds the bundle when a source tree has none (a compile wipes `out/`),
hands the webview `settings.js` and `settings.css` under a content-security policy that allows nothing else, and answers three
messages — `ready` with one state, `setting` with a key to write, `command` to run. A refresh is a new state and never a new
document, so a write does not throw away the reader's scroll. `npm run build-webview` builds it; `build-webviews` and
`vscode:prepublish` run that too.

## Design system

- **Fluent v9** (`@fluentui/react-components`, `@fluentui/react-icons`) with the brand ramp seeded from Azure blue
  `#0078D4` through `createLightTheme` / `createDarkTheme`, following VS Code's light, dark and high-contrast through
  `data-vscode-theme-kind`. The package named *Azure theme* (`@fluentui/azure-themes`) is v8/Fabric and does not apply.
- **Tokens**: spacing 4 / 8 / 12 / 16 / 24 · radius 4 / 6 / 10 · type 20 / 16 / 14 / 13 / 11 · row 22 (compact 20) ·
  status colours taken from the VS Code chart colours, so a chip, a dot and the status bar cannot disagree.
- **Row anatomy**, one component reused everywhere: `[icon] title · meta · status dot · hover actions`.
- **Page anatomy**: the title block (one `h1`, one line of why, the ways in), then sections whose titles carry a 2px
  brand bar to their left, then at most **one** tinted callout, which is the thing a person would do next. Figures live
  beside the thing they are about — a stage of a pipeline, a row in a list — never in a strip of tiles whose only label
  is the number itself.
- **A list folds, it does not truncate**: the first five rows are shown and the rest are behind a native
  `<details><summary>…</summary>`, so the count in the heading stays true.

## What was decided

| question | decision |
|---|---|
| activity bar | five: Home · Docs · AI · Tools · Settings |
| left panel | Fluent header, search, chips and footer in a webview view; the Pages tree stays native |
| build | our own generator, so a project with only `.md` files builds; a project's own `docs:site` still wins |
| deploy | build in the editor, push to `gh-pages` with a plain (never forced) push; the one-time Pages switch via `gh` when available |
| export | site `.zip`, single-file HTML, Markdown bundle; `.docx` optional |
| order of work | generator → deploy → export → panel and shell |

## Order of work

1. **Generator** ✅ — `src/siteRender.ts` (pure: slugs, page shell, index, sitemap, one stylesheet), `src/site.ts` (host:
   read, render, write, copy media), `scripts/check-site.mts` (10 cases); `Build the Site` falls back to it.
2. **Deploy** ✅ — `src/pages.ts` (the remote decides the sitemap's address) and `src/git.ts` shared with the diff command.
   The push of a site lives in `temco-learn` (`src/publish.ts`: `ensureRepo` refuses a folder that is not a clone, then a
   plain, never-forced `git push origin HEAD:main`; `scripts/check-publish.mts`, 5 cases against real throwaway
   repositories);
   `temcoDocs.publishSite` stays behind as a redirect. Still owed: the status-bar state and the confirmation screen S12.
3. **Export** ✅ — `src/zip.ts` (a real archive writer: store, deflate, CRC32), `src/export.ts` (site zip, single-file
   page, Markdown bundle; `temcoDocs.export` + three commands), `scripts/check-export.mts` (5 cases that read the archive
   back). Still owed: `.docx`. Then the panel.
4. **Panel and shell** — *(in progress)* the activity bar is five entries ✅; the **Project** strip above the native Pages
   tree ✅ (`src/panelView.ts`, `check-panel.mts`); the **status bar** ✅ (`src/statusLine.ts`, `check-status.mts`); **S3**
   (the project dashboard) ✅ inside the Project Detail page: pages · drafts · stale · issues, a *Needs you* group, and
   *Last out of the building*; **S13** ✅ as the **Exports** page (`temcoDocs.export` opens it; the three shapes are still
   their own commands, plus **`.docx`** from `src/docxWrite.ts` — Word's format is a zip of XML, so it needs no dependency);
   **S12** ✅ folded into the Releases page as *Where It Publishes*; **S1** ✅ the Projects page opens itself when the window
   is on no project and has no tab; **S6** ✅ as *The Registry* page — every entry with the reason it might not appear in the
   site, and one action per reason; **S10** ✅ the generator reports its steps (Resolve → Render → Assets → Sitemap) to the
   notification and the output channel as they happen; **S11** ✅ publishing a release confirms first, naming version,
   destination and file count.

   **Phase 4 is done, and so is every gap the screen audit found.** What remains for the shell is the tabbed *Ship* surface
   (Build · Release · Deploy · Export as one page) — deliberately not built: after the cross-links and the Exports page, the
   four surfaces point at each other, so it is polish rather than a gap.
   The status line deliberately carries the window's facts (project · pages · issues) and not the version or the last build:
   those need the catalog, and a catalog read on every file save is too high a price for a status bar. The strip in the side
   bar shows them instead, from a render that already had a reason to read the catalog.

**Overview is an application page** (`src/overview.ts`, view type `temcoLearn.overview`), drawn with `temco-ui` the way every
other page of ours is, and it is never published. It opens with the sentence that says what the module is for — *Temco Learn
publishes the documentation website* — continued by the counts it reads from the snapshot (*3 projects are published today, 7
versions and 42 pages between them*) so no project name is ever written into the prose. Then, in this order: one callout for what
needs doing (or that nothing does, or that nothing has been built yet); **Publishing workflow**, four cards — *Build · Add ·
Publish · Download* — one line each, ending in the link that performs it (`Open Temco Docs`, `Choose a build · 1 waiting`,
`Publish · 3 changes`, `Show in the folder`); and **Where it stands**, three lines, one per page that acts on the facts they
name. The section headings carry the 2 px brand bar the other pages use.

**And it stops there — with one line back.** The page used to go on with three parts — *Published projects*, *Build outputs* and
*Publication* — that restated what the pages which act on those facts already say: the Site page holds the clone, the versions it
serves and where it is published; the Artifacts page holds what a build wrote; the Publishing page holds the address, the branch and
what a push would carry. A summary page that copies the page it summarises drifts from it, so the **lists** went. What came back is
**Where it stands**, and it is deliberately one line per page rather than a section per subject:

| line | the fact on it | it opens |
|---|---|---|
| `Documentation Site` | `2 projects · 3 versions · 4 pages` | the Site page |
| `Artifacts` | `3 files · 5.3 MB` | the Artifacts page |
| `Publishing` | `https://temcocontrols.github.io/docs/ · 3 changes waiting` | the Publishing page |

A **count** is the one thing worth repeating: it is computed from the same snapshot on every draw, so it cannot drift, and it
answers "is there anything in there?" without a list that has to be kept in step. Each line ends in the standard `Open ›`, so the
detail is one click away and lives where it is edited.

**Add a build** is a section of the **Site page**, not a panel view: it belongs to the page that clones, builds, runs and
publishes the site, because it writes into the same repository those do. `Builds not on the site` lists every build on disk
whose project is absent from the catalog or whose `dist/site` is newer than the version the catalog lists, each with the
reason (`not on the site`, `newer than 13.0`) and one `Add…` button. Adding asks for the version, copies into
`public/imported/`, runs `npm run catalog`, and publishes nothing. Every command that means "add a build" opens that page with
the section first and marked. Nothing about it is automatic: a test build stays out of the public site until somebody adds it.
A link on this page is text with a glyph (`temcoLearn.*` command), never an `href` into
the website — that boundary was crossed once already. The module therefore depends on `temcocontrols.temco-ui`, exactly as
`temco-docs` does.

A build is not one thing. `temco-docs` writes the site (`dist/docs` or `dist/site`), an offline help file (`.chm`, inside the
repository or in a `HelpDocs` folder beside it), often a `.pdf`, and the exports it keeps in `dist/` — a zip of the site, a Word
document, single pages. `src/artifacts.ts` reads those three places per project and reports what is there: nothing is invented, so
a project with no `.chm` simply has no `.chm` row, and a file in a shared `HelpDocs` folder belongs to the project it is named
after. Each artifact is either something to look at (`site`, `chm`, `pdf`, a single page) or something to take away (`zip`, `docx`,
and the files that are both), which is the divide the panel draws.

The panel (`src/tree.ts`) is three views in the order the questions are asked — **Overview** (one row: the page above),
**Site** (`Preview the Site` with a state dot and the port, and **Library** with one row per project at its newest version),
and **Artifacts** (a project, and the files its build wrote — names only, with the size and the path on hover; a row saves a copy and offers *Show in
the Folder* inline; `Publish the Site` closes the view, its count at the row's right end). What a row holds is its `description`:
the grey words beside a name, as the Explorer writes them — and where a row deliberately says nothing, the hover carries it.



---

---
title: "The authoring flow"
order: 403
edit: false
---

> **Superseded for the side bar layout.** The Docs side bar (Pages, Needs You, Sources, Publish) is defined in [redesign.md](redesign). This page remains the record for the screenshot capture stage only.

The design for **screenshots in the authoring loop**, and for the side bar that had to change because of them. This is the record the next round argues with, so that a redesign starts from a decision rather than from the previous screenshot.

## The reference

The bar is **Dr.Explain**, and its loop is not "a documentation editor with an image button":

> capture a *running window* → its controls become numbered callouts → write one line of text per number → export the same topics to CHM, PDF and the web.

Two properties make that work: the screenshot is taken *of a window*, so the callouts can be placed by the machine instead of by hand; and every artifact is built from one set of topics. Before this change we had no capture stage at all, so the side bar had filled the space with destinations that were not places in the documentation — Projects, Areas, Pipeline, Working Set.

## The flow

```
 T3000 is running — the thing being documented
     │  Ctrl+Alt+S  capture a screen          Ctrl+Alt+V  paste from the clipboard
     ▼
 CAPTURE     the screen becomes a file
     ▼
 TOPIC       the page being written; the file lands in that page's own media/
     │       NN-area-slug.png · ≥ 1600 px · ≤ 2 MB · type sniffed, never trusted from the name
     ▼
 TEXT        the body, with the reference at the cursor
     ▼
 GATE        registry · links · media audit · width · the version a page describes
     ▼
 BUILD       the product repo's own scripts → the site, and the offline help
     ▼
 NEW VERSION version-report.json → "retake these" rows → Ctrl+Alt+V again
```

Stages 1 and 3–5 exist and are untouched by this change. **Capture was the missing stage**, and it is now built as far as it can be without the callout decision below.

## The rule that places every feature

| The question a feature answers | Where it lives |
|---|---|
| "Where in the docs am I?" | the **Pages** tree |
| "What am I writing right now?" | the **page** and the editor's toolbar |
| "What is waiting for me?" | **Needs You** |
| "What am I shipping?" | one Build & Release page, reached from the tree's title bar |
| none of the above | it does not belong in the product |

## What changed

**The tree gained a home of its own; the Documentation panel kept the groups it had.**

| Surface | What it is |
|---|---|
| **Documentation** panel (`temco`) | Projects · Pages · Areas · Pipeline · Working Set — as before |
| **Documentation Tree** (new activity-bar container, `temcoDocsTree`) | **Pages** — the documentation tree, the same provider as the panel's Pages view |
| | **Needs You** — the queue below it: screenshots with no page yet, pages the code moved away from, pages never reviewed |

One provider behind both tree views, so there is still one answer to "what is the documentation". The menus on the panel's Pages view apply to the tree view as well.

**Needs You** lists only facts something else already computes, and every row runs a command that already existed:

- screenshots with no page yet, from the capture inbox;
- pages the product repo marks `stale` (read, never recomputed);
- pages never reviewed, as one row with a count and the report behind it.

**Screenshots are a stage.** `temcoDocs.captureImage` (Ctrl+Alt+S) opens the Windows snip overlay and takes what it puts on the clipboard; `temcoDocs.pasteImage` (Ctrl+Alt+V) takes an image that is already there. Both then:

1. find the page in front — the active editor's page, else the row the tree has selected;
2. write into that page's `media/` as `NN-slug.ext`, the number continuing the folder's own sequence;
3. insert `![title](media/NN-slug.ext)` at the cursor, or at the end of the page when it was not open;
4. warn — not refuse — below 1600 px wide, and refuse outright above 2 MB.

With no page in front the screen is not guessed at: it goes to `<root>/.temco/captures/` and waits in **Needs You** until *Attach the Screenshot to a Page…* puts it where a person says. The inbox is outside every `media/`, so the site only ever holds what a page shows.

`temcoDocs.chooseImage` is the third path, for a file that is already on disk; it is the one that also works outside Windows.

### Why reading the clipboard needed no change to the core

The extension API reads the clipboard's **text** and not its image — `docs/temco-platform/ui/editor-design.md` §3 said so, and it is still true. But this application is Windows, and `System.Windows.Forms.Clipboard` reads the image with no native module and nothing installed: what Win+Shift+S, PrintScreen, Snipping Tool and every other capture tool leave behind. So the limit is a platform fact rather than a wall, and it costs one PowerShell child process per capture.

## What is not built, and the decision it waits for

**Annotation.** Crop, arrow, box, text and blur/redact, saved over the capture, with the untouched original kept aside so a retake is diffable. It is a contained piece (a canvas over the captured image), but it contradicts `15-online-editor.md`, *What is not built* — "a screenshot is captured properly or not at all" — so it is a rule change that needs to be taken deliberately, not slipped in with a button.

**Automatic callouts**, the Dr.Explain core. It depends entirely on what is captured:

| What the screen is of | What can be placed automatically |
|---|---|
| our own webview T3000 | callouts from the real DOM elements — this is Dr.Explain's magic, nearly free |
| the classic Win32 T3000 | control bounds from Windows UI Automation — automatic, but coarse |
| a device screen, or a customer's machine | nothing; manual crop, arrow, box, blur is the whole feature |

The answer decides whether the annotator is a callout tool or a drawing tool, which is why it comes before the annotator.

**The version report.** `docs/help/version-report.json` — version, changed areas, affected pages, images older than the version they describe — written by the release workflow, read by **Needs You** and by Build & Release, consumed by the MCP server. Designed, not built; it needs no UI of its own.

## Order

1. **The two views** — done.
2. **Capture, paste and the inbox** — done.
3. **The callout decision**, then annotation in the page.
4. **Build & Release** as one page: the build, review and release commands that exist today, in one place.
5. **`version-report.json`**, then the Action and the MCP wrapper.


---

---
title: "The editor"
order: 404
edit: false
---

The design for **R6 — editing and publishing**: the toolbar, creating a document, images, drafts, and where the
review happens. It is written the same week its first step was built, so it separates what exists from what is
proposed, and it puts the one decision that is not mine in front of the person who has to make it.

The content rules are not here. Registry schema, link forms, media rules, review flow and publishing belong to
`design/temco-learn/`, which stays the source of truth; this document only says how the desktop application
implements them.

---

## 1. The surface: VS Code's editor, with the project's toolbar on it

`15-online-editor.md` designs the **hosted editor**: Monaco, bundled and self-hosted, with an API behind
it, served by the center tool on a host that has a clone. That editor is not this application's editor and
building it twice would be the bug.

| | the hosted editor | this application |
|---|---|---|
| the core | Monaco, bundled by us, ~2 MB, custom build, `<textarea>` fallback | **VS Code is Monaco plus a shell** — nothing to bundle |
| what it adds | toolbar, preview pane, gate markers, upload, publish, PR | toolbar, a link to a real page, the outline, the gates' markers — of which the markers already exist (`src/diagnostics.ts`) |
| what it never does | never WYSIWYG: a rich round-trip rewrites the line layout of the whole file | the same rule, and the same reason |
| publishing | `POST /api/docs/publish` → a real PR | **never.** This application builds and reports; shipping stays a separate act |

So the desktop equivalent of "Monaco + the toolbar + completion + markers" is **VS Code, with the toolbar built**
— which is what `src/format.ts` now is. An editor page of the project's own, hosting Monaco in a webview, was considered and
is refused for the reason `15-online-editor.md`, *What is not built* gives for the website: the integration is the part that matters,
and the editor is the part that already exists.

### What exists — the toolbar, built

Sixteen commands, all `temcoDocs.format.*`, in `src/format.ts`. Each one edits the buffer in front: the page a
person is typing in, undone with `Ctrl+Z`. None of them writes a file, opens a tab, or touches the registry.

| Where a person meets it | What is there |
|---|---|
| the editor's title bar, Markdown files only | **Bold** · *Italic* · *Link to a page* · **Format** |
| the editor's context menu, and the tab's | the submenu **Temco Docs** — all sixteen, grouped |
| the palette | every action, by name; **Format** lists them all in one picker |

| Group | Actions |
|---|---|
| Inline | Bold `**…**` · Italic `*…*` · Inline code `` `…` `` |
| Blocks | Heading level (`##`–`####`) · bullet · numbered · task · block quote · code block with a language · table · horizontal rule · callout |
| Insert | Link · **Link to a page** |
| Navigate | Go to a heading (this page's outline) |

Three things it does on purpose, each of which has an obvious wrong answer:

- **Every button is a toggle.** Bold over `**bold**` removes the marks; the bullet list over a list takes the
  bullets off; the same heading level again removes it. That is what a button does in every other editor.
- **A block gets a blank line before it when the line above is prose.** Not decoration: `text` followed by `---` is
  a level-2 heading in Markdown, and a table glued to a paragraph is a paragraph.
- **A callout, a fence and an alert marker start their own line** — mid-line they would glue to the text after the
  cursor, and `> [!NOTE]` is an alert only at the start of a line.

**No keybindings are contributed, deliberately.** `Ctrl+B` hides the side bar everywhere else, and this extension
has to keep working in an ordinary VS Code. Everything here is a command, so a chord is one personal binding away.

**A page that is not a file on disk says so.** A page read from a source, or a diff, has a scheme that is not
`file`, and every action refuses it in words rather than appearing to work.

---

## 2. Creating a document

**A page is a file, not a pair — and that is the product repository's own doing.** This section said the
opposite when it was first written, so the correction is worth stating plainly, with where it comes from.
`T3000Webview-EEZ-Studio/scripts/docs-registry.mjs` **generates the entries**:

```
  buildPages(existing)                    walks docs/t3000/** and docs/legacy/**
    id      = the docs-relative path without `.md`      the page id *is* the path
    title   = prev.title  ??  the file's first `#`      ← so a skeleton starts with one
    area    = the top folder      chapter = the second path segment (or `(root)`)
    order   = prev.order  ??  10, 20, 30… inside the chapter
    owner   = 'docs'   ship = true for t3000   tags/keywords/code = []
    every field:  prev.<field> ?? a default             ← an existing (curated) value always wins
  and the file itself is written with  JSON.stringify(registry, null, 2) + '\n'
```

So **creating a page writes no entry at all**: the file goes in, the repository's own generator — which this
application already runs as one of its allowlisted scripts — adds the entry, derives its place, and takes its
title from the first heading. What only a person curates is `order`, `chapter`, `title`, `owner`, `tags` and
`code`, and those are the values the generator then leaves alone. Section 5 is that decision, as taken.

Two consequences for the flow below, both now built: a new page is **a file and a generator run**, offered in
that order; and there is **no "loose page" kind**, because the `exclude` key the design package's schema
describes is not produced by this repository's generator — a Markdown file under `docs/` that no area covers is
an `unregistered page` error, not a working note.

### The kinds worth offering

| Kind | The headings it starts with | Why it is a kind |
|---|---|---|
| **Article** | Overview · Configuration · Related pages | the ordinary case |
| **Task** | Before you start · Steps · If it does not work | the commonest documentation shape |
| **Reference** | Fields · Notes | the shape an API or a register actually has |
| **Troubleshooting** | Symptom · Cause · Fix | matches the `troubleshooting` area the product already has |
| **Release note** | What is new · Fixed · Known issues | what changed in a build |

Each skeleton is **headings only** — no invented sentences, because a template that writes prose is a template
someone has to delete. Every one starts with `# <title>`, which is what the generator reads.

### The flow, as built

```
  New page ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  1  Where    the row it was started from — a page's own area and chapter, a chapter itself,
              an area's root chapter — or the row you have selected, or:
              which source ▸ which area ▸ which chapter
  2  Kind     article · task · reference · troubleshooting · release note
  3  Title    Trend Logs
  4  File     trend-logs          →  docs/t3000/features/trend-logs.md        (shown, not guessed)
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [ Write the page ]  ── it starts as “# Trend Logs” and the kind's headings
  ── then ──
  “It becomes a page when the registry names it.”          [ Run the generator ]
```

A repository row cannot answer step 1 — it is not in an area — so it answers the source and the rest is still
asked. Nothing else is asked when the kind came from the *New Page of a Kind* submenu: step 2 is the entry that
was clicked, and steps 3 and 4 follow as they always do.

**New chapter** is the same flow with the folder named first, because a chapter is a page's *path*: a folder
with nothing in it is not a chapter, not in the registry, and not in the table of contents. The folder arrives
with its first page.

---

## 3. Images

An image is the one action in the toolbar that is missing, and the reason is not the code: the file has to be
written into the page's own `media/` folder before `![alt](media/…)` points at anything.

The rules are already written down, in the design package, so they are not ours to invent:

| Rule | Source |
|---|---|
| the file goes into the **page's own `media/`**, with a slugified name (`02-run-screen.png`) | `15-online-editor.md`, *Uploads* |
| accepted: PNG, JPG, WebP, GIF, SVG — diagrams as SVG, sanitised on the way in | `15-online-editor.md`, *Uploads* |
| images ≤ 2 MB · a page ≤ 1 MB · content-type sniffed, never trusted from the name | `15-online-editor.md`, *Safety rules*, rule 2 |
| **the destination is derived, never supplied** | `15-online-editor.md`, *Safety rules*, rule 1 |
| screenshots are captured properly or not at all — no editing, no resizing | `15-online-editor.md`, *What is not built* |

**What an extension can and cannot take.** A file the user **picked** (`showOpenDialog`), a file **dropped** on
the page and an image already on the clipboard are all reachable — the last one not through the extension API,
which exposes the clipboard's *text* only. On Windows the module reads the bitmap itself
(`System.Windows.Forms.Clipboard`, one child process per capture), which is what makes `Ctrl+Alt+V` work; the loop
it belongs to, and what is still missing from it, is `authoring-flow.md`.

---

## 4. Drafts, review and publishing

**Not built, and the largest piece of R6.** The design is already fixed and is not ours to change:

| | where it belongs |
|---|---|
| editing a **local** source | in place — the page file is the page, which the Workspace view already does |
| editing a **git** source | on a draft branch in a materialised clone — which is the `fetch` read mode R2 still owes |
| review | `docs:blocking`, `registry --check`, the link check and the media check, run by the repository's own scripts |
| publishing | never from this application: the gates, the commit and the PR are the product repository's |
| a page's fields in the editor | restricted by `15-online-editor.md`, *Safety rules*, rule 4: structure needs the docs owner |

So the honest order is: **images and create-a-page first, because both need only a decision**; drafts after, because
they need the `fetch` mode and a review flow that lives in the product repository.

---

## 5. The decision, as taken

The question was how far this application may go into a product repository. The answer, in the three parts it
was asked in:

| Asked | Taken | So the code does this |
|---|---|---|
| The Library's title bar holds twelve buttons | **three inline, the rest in the view's `⋯`** | `newProject` · `openProjects` · `openNeeds` stay in group `navigation`; everything else moved to named groups, which is how a `view/title` item lands in the overflow menu. **The header starts work; a row and a page operate on a thing** — and the target-less ones ask which, which they already did |
| Which operations the Workspace tree grows | **all five**: new page · new chapter · rename the title · move (up, down, and by dragging) · retire | `src/pageOps.ts` — six commands, a `TreeDragAndDropController`, and one modal per write |
| How far into `registry.json` | **one named value, on a confirmation** | `src/registryEdit.ts` — `title`, `order`, `chapter`, `deprecated`, `superseded_by`, and nothing else; never a whole-file rewrite |

Where a row offers its operations was settled on the third attempt: **the row menu is the Explorer's, the toolbar
mirrors it, and the hover carries nothing.** A **left click** does what it does in the Explorer — opens a page,
expands a folder — and a **right click** (or the keyboard's menu key) opens *New Page… · New Chapter… · New Page
of a Kind ▸ · Reveal in Explorer · Open in Integrated Terminal · Copy Path · Copy Relative Path · Rename the
Title… · Move the Page Up · Move the Page Down · Retire… · Open the Registry*, each item only where it means
something. The view's `…` shows the same list — plus *New page*, *New chapter*, *Refresh*, Home and Validate —
acting on the row the tree last had focused, which is also what makes it usable from the palette: **a view title
action is run with the tree pane's context**, and every row command falls back to the view's own selection. There
are no hover icons on a row: a menu started from a row can only ever be a quick pick in the middle of the top of
the window, and that is exactly what the row menu above replaces.

### What each operation writes

```
 operation                        what changes on disk                          state
 ──────────────────────────────── ───────────────────────────────────────────── ──────────────
 Open · Reveal · the outline       nothing                                      built
 Create the missing page file      one empty .md (declared, absent)             built (earlier)
 New page in this chapter          one .md from a kind's skeleton               built
   → then the repo's docs:registry (its own script; adds the entry)             offered, not forced
 New chapter                       …the same, with the folder named first       built
 Rename the title                  title in registry.json (+ the page's first #) built
 Move up / Move down / drag        order in registry.json — two numbers, swapped built
 Drop on another chapter           chapter + order — *structure*, docs owner     built
 Retire a page                     deprecated + superseded_by                    built
 Rename or move the FILE           the page id — inbound links break            **never**, by rule
 An image into the page's media/   a new file, binary                            **not built** — see the API in `15-online-editor.md`
```

Four rules hold the writes together, and each one is a decision rather than an implementation detail:

- **A move is a swap, never a renumbering.** Placing a page between two others by rewriting every `order` in the
  chapter is the generator's own job — it hands out 10, 20, 30… to whatever nobody curated. So a move writes two
  numbers and the diff is two lines.
- **The file is never renamed or moved.** The page id *is* the path (`05-content-and-registry.md`, *Page ids and aliases*), so a page
  is renamed by changing what it is *called*: one string in the registry, plus the page's own `#` heading when it
  says the same words — two answers to one question otherwise.
- **A chapter change says the word structure.** `15-online-editor.md`, *Safety rules*, rule 4 marks `chapter` as the docs owner's to
  change, so the confirmation says so and asks for a line in the pull request.
- **Retirement is not deletion.** The file stays; the registry records the replacement; the redirect for a
  retired page is generated by the repository's own pipeline (`14-governance.md`, *Deprecation and retirement*).

### The evidence for "one value, and nothing else"

`src/registryEdit.ts` renders the file the way the repository's **own generator** writes it — `JSON.stringify`
with two spaces, one trailing newline — **and with the file's own line endings**, because a clone checked out on
Windows is CRLF while the generator emits LF, and a write that flipped every ending would be a diff of the whole
file. With both right, the guarantee is testable, so it was tested:

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| the real `docs/help/registry.json` — 318 pages, 124 KB — opened, re-rendered, compared | **byte-for-byte identical** |
| a drag into another chapter, diffed line by line against the original | **two lines changed**: `chapter` and `order` |
| a drag onto a sibling, same chapter | two lines changed, the two `order` values swapped |
| a drop on the page's own chapter | **nothing written**, and it says so |
| a rename, diffed | one line changed, plus the page's own heading |
| a new page | the file written, **the registry untouched**, then the generator offered |

Running the generator afterwards is what keeps the file canonical: the values written here are the ones the
generator's own `prev.<field> ?? default` rule preserves, so nothing is lost by it and nothing here duplicates it.

---

## 6. Acceptance, per step

| # | Step | The test that ends it |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | the toolbar | **done** — a Markdown file shows the four buttons; every button toggles; `---` after prose is not a heading; the page picker inserts a link the gate resolves; a page read from a source is refused in words |
| 2 | operating on a page | **done** — a page is created from its kind's skeleton and the generator is offered next; a title is renamed and the registry diff is one line; a page is dragged into another chapter and the diff is the two values; retiring one writes `deprecated` and the replacement and keeps the file |
| 3 | insert an image | a dropped PNG lands in the page's `media/` under a slugified name and is referenced relatively; a 3 MB PNG and a `.exe` renamed `.png` are both refused, in words, with the limit |
| 4 | drafts | a page is corrected, the gates run, the diff is reviewed, and the result is a commit a person made — not one this application made |


---

---
title: "The platform"
order: 405
edit: false
---

The center documentation platform. One internal application — VS Code plus this extension — that takes
documentation from **many kinds of source**, lets people manage and edit it, and **builds the final
documents**: a site, a CHM, a PDF. A local AI reads the history of those sources and drafts the updates.
Everything it knows lives in a local **SQLite catalog**.

This document is the platform's own design. The **content** rules — the registry schema, the checks, the
review flow, the publishing engine — are the design package in `design/temco-learn/`, which stays the
source of truth.

## How Microsoft's own documentation is managed, for comparison

Worth stating, because it is the shape being copied:

- **A docset** is a set of Markdown pages plus a table of contents, living in a repository (Microsoft's own
  docs are a private repo of Markdown).
- **Metadata** on each page — `author`, `ms.topic`, `ms.date` — decides where it appears and who owns it.
- **Monikers** are version markers: one page can serve several product versions, and the build emits the
  right one per version.
- **A build pipeline** turns the docset into the published site, and separately into an offline PDF.
- **The reader** gets one searchable site; the *authoring* side (review, status, ownership) is internal.

The equivalents: docset ≈ a **Document**, moniker ≈ its **version**, pipeline ≈ its **recipe**. The difference
is where it runs: theirs is a hosted service, ours is an internal app on the maintainer's machine — and ours
must also produce the **CHM**, which theirs does not.

## The model: sources → documents → artifacts

The unit of work is **not a repository**. A repository is where content may *start*; the thing a person creates,
owns, versions and builds is a **document** — "T3000 Developer Help", shipped as `t3000_dev.chm`; "T3000Webview
Guide", shipped as `t3000_webview.pdf`.

```
  SOURCES  (many kinds — where content comes from)          DOCUMENTS  (what a person creates and manages)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐              ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ git     T3000Webview @ main              │              │ T3000 Developer Help          v1.4     │
  │ git     T3_Engine     @ release/2.1      │ ─── cites ──►│   sources: the first two               │
  │ folder  C:\docs\t3000-extra              │              │   recipe:  chm + pdf + site            │
  │ folder  C:\docs\legacy (markdown tree)   │              │   pages:   318   owner: docs           │
  │ archive a supplier's PDF, read-only      │              │   version: 1.4   audience: developers  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘              └──────────────┬─────────────────────────┘
                    │                                                      │ build
                    │ materialise / read                                   ▼
                    │                                    ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
                    └──────► AI over history ────────────►│ ARTIFACTS  t3000_dev.chm  1.2 MB  ✔    │
                             summary · pages · drafts    │            t3000_dev.pdf  4.8 MB  ✔    │
                                                         │            the package  published ✔    │
                                                         └────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Source** — where content comes from. Not necessarily GitHub, not necessarily a repository, not necessarily
Markdown:

| Kind | Examples | What the platform does |
|---|---|---|
| `git` | any remote: GitHub, GitLab, a bare path — `owner/repo@branch` | reads the tree, the history and the diffs |
| `folder` | a docfx project, a Markdown tree, loose HTML, images | reads it in place; never moves it |
| `archive` | a supplier's PDF, a vendor's ZIP of pages | inventories it, read-only, for reference and search |

The **branch is chosen per source**, defaulting to the repository's own default (`main`), and reported when the
documentation is not on it.

**Document** — what a person creates: a name, its sources and their branches, a **recipe** (how to build it),
a **version**, an audience, an owner, and the outputs it must produce. This is the platform's unit of
management, and it is what the generator, the builds, the health and the AI are all *about*.

**Artifact** — a file a build produced: path, size, time, hash, and which document and source revision it came
from.

**Build** — one run of a document's recipe at a source revision: a job with a log, a status, and the artifacts
it left behind. Success is an artifact that did not exist before, never an exit code.

**Analysis** — what the local AI did over the history: a summary of a commit, the pages it affects, a draft,
and what is still owed.

## The catalog

The platform's memory: documents, sources, builds, artifacts, commits and what changed since you last looked.
Relational, long-lived, queryable offline.

**Where:** `context.globalStorageUri` of the **store module** — a `catalog.db` and a `sources/` cache of materialised trees. One window, one file, opened by the module that owns it: a caller that opened it with its own storage would find an empty database beside its own code.
**Never inside a repository**, never committed, never in a product's git history.

```sql
sources     id · kind(git|folder|archive) · name · remote_url · path · default_branch · branch
            last_fetch_at · last_commit_sha · last_commit_at · size_bytes · state

documents   id · name · slug · version · audience · owner · recipe(kind) · created_at · updated_at

doc_sources document_id · source_id · include_glob · branch · role(primary|supplement)

recipes     document_id · engine(docfx|own-script|chm|pdf|site) · config_path · script · outputs_json

builds      id · document_id · started_at · finished_at · status · revision · log_path

artifacts   id · build_id · document_id · path · bytes · built_at · sha256 · kind(chm|pdf|site|zip)

commits     sha · source_id · at · author · subject · summary · summary_model · summarised_at

commit_pages  commit_sha · page_id · how(code-glob|path|terms)

pages       id · document_id · source_id · path · title · owner · reviewed_at · stale · counts_json

scans       id · source_id · at · mode(local|fetch|api) · revision · counts_json

events      id · at · kind · subject_kind · subject_id · detail · seen_at
            kind ∈ { new_source, docs_found, registry_found, push, pages_changed,
                     docs_removed, branch_mismatch, build_ok, build_failed, draft_ready }
```

Every view is a query over these rows. Nothing is asked of the network twice, and the last scan is readable
with the network unplugged — and says when it was taken.

## Reading a source: three ways, and when to use which

The user's requirement — *"load the source code… download to a local temp folder or read it from online"* —
is two mechanisms with different costs, and the platform picks per job:

| Mode | How | Used for | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| **local** | a folder you have open, or a path in the catalog | everything: files, checks, edits, builds | none |
| **materialised** | a shallow fetch into `globalStorage/sources/<slug>` — `git clone --filter=blob:none --depth 1`, or a fetch of the needed range — on a scratch branch the platform owns | drafts (the model needs the page text), builds, file-existence checks | disk, bounded |
| **online** | the host's API for one commit's changed files and patches | commit summaries, "what changed" — *diffs* only, no clone | API calls, bounded |

The rule that picks between the last two: **summaries need diffs; drafts need files.** A commit summary over
an API patch is cheap and needs no clone; a draft needs the real page, so the platform materialises the source
once and reuses it. A patch can be truncated for a very large file — the summary records that it was, rather
than guessing.

**Caps, because this is a maintainer's PC:** a source cache budget (default 2 GB) with LRU eviction, a maximum
fetch depth, a maximum commits analysed per run, and a *Prune the source cache* command. Nothing is ever
fetched without a command or a queue the user started.

## The AI over history, per source

```
   commits since the last scan   ──►  summarise what it did, in the product's words
       (API patches, or the local clone)      │
                                              ├──► which pages it affects
                                              │      code globs from the registry, else path, else terms
                                              ├──► draft the page edits  (local model, draft-only)
                                              └──► add to the owed list
   ▲  nothing here publishes, commits or approves: a named person does that
```

Summaries, their model and their time are stored, so a summary is computed once and is visible offline. The
whole pass is a **job with progress** — resumable, bounded per run, and honest about what it skipped.

## Creating a document

*Create a document* asks for the few facts the catalog needs, and nothing more:

```
   name        T3000 Developer Help
   sources     [x] T3000Webview  branch main        [x] C:\docs\t3000-extra
               [ ] T3_Engine     branch release/2.1
   recipe      ( ) site   ( ) pdf   (x) chm   [x] also the site
   version     1.4            audience  developers            owner  docs
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   the platform answers with what it can see:  318 pages · 5 sources · 2 sources carry a registry
                                                suggested: run the T3000Webview generator first
```

A document can be created before its sources are perfect — that is the point. The platform records what it
found, says what is missing, and offers the next step.

## Editing and writing

- **A local source is edited in place** — the page file is the page, as the Content view already does.
- **A git source is edited on a draft branch** in the materialised clone: the AI's diff is applied there, the
  user reviews it, and *they* commit or open a PR. The platform never commits on anyone's behalf.
- **Nothing under `_admin/` or `_edit/` is ever written** — `00-architecture.md` calls those *generated pages,
  not documentation.*
- The hosted editor stays the design package's web surface; this is its desktop
  equivalent.

## Telling the user — quietly

Unseen events become **one** grouped notification, never one per event, and `seen_at` is stamped when the group
is shown or dismissed so a fact is never announced twice. Default `docs-only`: a daily summary of documentation
change, no modals. A *Since you last looked* node heads the view.

## Feasibility

**Yes.** It is a catalog, a few connectors, a build runner and the AI that already exists — no new engine, and
no server. The real constraints, each with its answer:

| Constraint | Answer |
|---|---|
| **The database** — decided **`node:sqlite`**: it ships with the extension host's Node, so there is no dependency to add | a build whose Node predates 22.5 is refused in words by the store, at the point of use. The `engines.vscode` floor should be raised once the first F5 confirms the built-in is present |
| **Disk** — cached source trees grow | a budget (2 GB default), LRU eviction, *Prune the source cache*, and a `.gitignore`-aware size report |
| **The network** — a whole-organisation read and commit patches need rate limit | a token in secret storage (5,000 calls/hour), and *online* mode on demand only. The 60-call limit has been reached in practice; it is not an optional convenience |
| **Time** — AI summaries over history are slow | a job queue with progress, a per-run cap, resumable, and summaries stored once |
| **Many kinds of local documentation** | one ingester per kind, starting with: a Markdown tree, a docfx project, and an inventory-only reader for HTML and PDF |
| **Trust** — this is the maintainer's machine | all state in `globalStorageUri`; nothing written into a repository; nothing fetched without a command or a queue the user started |

## Surfaces

The platform has **two lists** in the sidebar and **rich pages** in the editor area. Nothing else competes for
attention: a document is managed from its page, a source is read from its row, and the AI is beside whatever
you are working on.

**What opens when the platform is activated is the project you are on** — its dashboard, the one surface that
says what is in it and what next — then the page you were last working on, then the home page when there is
neither. `temcoWorkbench.start.open` (`project` · `page` · `home` · `welcome` · `none`) chooses, and `none` opens no tab at
all for anyone who would rather nothing opened by itself. The side panel the window is revealed on is a second,
separate choice — `temcoWorkbench.start.panel`, whose values are the left panel's own items (Home, Documentation,
Repositories, AI, Forum, Tools, Settings) and whose default is `temcoDashboard`.

**The Workspace is the first view, and the Library is beneath it.** Both sit in the `temco` activity bar: the
Workspace tree is the documentation module's, the Library is the workbench's — the one index of everything every
module has. The Workspace shows the project being
worked on and never the open folders, so the registry checks, the status bar and the faults follow the project
too — a tree built from whatever happens to be open would be a second, unrelated answer to the same question.
The view is never hidden and never blank: with nothing to show it carries **rows that say why and offer the one
act that fills it** (*no project yet* → create one; *no project is being worked on* → choose one in the Library;
*this project has nothing to show* → open it and read its sources). Those rows are drawn by the tree itself —
`viewsWelcome` was tried first and dropped, because a welcome's `when` clause is resolved when the view is first
painted, which is before the refresh has read anything: it announced "nothing recorded yet" over a catalog that
held five projects.

With exactly one project recorded there is nothing to choose between, so it selects itself and the tree is ready
without anyone picking it; that is not a person choosing, so the sidebar is left where it was. Choosing a project
afterwards brings the Workspace forward, and clearing one brings the Library back — the two views are a sequence,
and only a click walks it.

**Nothing here is specific to a product.** The names in the figures below are examples; the code knows no
repository, no branch and no product name, and it reads whatever the catalog and the editor are told about.

```
  SIDEBAR (activity bar — the two lists)      EDITOR AREA (the pages)          RIGHT SIDE
  ┌──────────────┐                            ┌──────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ DOCUMENTS    │  what you manage           │ Document             │         │ TEMCO AI             │
  │ SOURCES      │  what you read from        │ Create a document    │         │  the AI, beside      │
  └──────────────┘                            │ AI analysis (job)    │         │  what you work on    │
                                              │ Build log            │         └──────────────────────┘
                                              └──────────────────────┘
```

### The project flow

The flow was reworked after the first attempt at it was judged — correctly — not good: a project's pages existed
in two places with two truths, a source could not be acted on from the row that named it, and *Add a source* threw
the whole edit form at you. What changed, in the order it matters:

```
  selected project ──────────► the Workspace view, the counts, the faults, the AI
        │                       (one world: the view follows the project, not the open folder)
        ├── Library row  ⚙ Read · Open the source · Set the branch · Forget     (actions on the row)
        ├── hub          Read every source · Add a source · Build · Edit        (the work, on one page)
        └── hub ▸ Sources  the add form lives here — a source is one decision, not the whole project
```

| # | What it was | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | the Workspace view showed the open folders; a project's pages lived in the catalog, beside it | **one world**: with a project being worked on, the view shows *that project's* sources — a local folder gets the full checks even when the window has it closed, and a source that is not here shows what the read recorded, marked `origin: 'catalog'` so nothing unchecked looks checked. With no project it says why instead of showing the folders |
| **B** | a Library row was text; reading was only on the view's title bar | rows carry a context value — **Read · Open the source · Set the branch · Forget** on a source, **Work on · Edit · Add a source · Build** on a project |
| **C** | *Read the sources* read **one** source and said "sources" | **Read every source** reads them all, one at a time (a rate-limited host is not asked twice at once), with one progress notice and one report |
| **D** | *Add a source* opened the whole edit form | the add form is on the hub's **Sources** tab: repository + branch, or a folder, or an archive |
| **E** | a build ran only the output script | a build runs the repository's own **`docs:registry`** first when it has one — that is what writes the index, the headings and the stale verdict — then the output script, and says which of the two it ran |
| **F** | the AI answered about the open folder only, and said nothing about what it was answering | the panel is **about the project**: it names it, and when no editor is in front it falls back to the page the project remembers you were on |

**The Library's *Projects* row** (`extensions/temco-workbench/src/views.ts`) is the list of what this application manages.
Each project's row carries what a project is made of and what can be done with it — the four technical facts are
on the row and its children, so nothing has to be opened to decide: version · owner · outputs; its sources with how
many pages each holds and how it was read; how many pages, how many without an owner, how many artifacts and when
the last one was built; and the operations — *Work on it* · *Open* · *Read sources* · *Build* · *Edit* · *Remove…*.
The one being worked on is first and marked, because the panel is read top-down.

**A page for a project that has been removed closes itself, and the projects page takes its place.** The first
version kept the tab and put a notice in it — which was wrong twice over. It cleared `document.body`, taking the
tabs, the actions and every element the next render writes into with it, so the tab could never become another
project's page; and it left a page *about nothing*, with working-looking buttons on it. A tab for something that
no longer exists is worse than no tab: the removal says so in one message, closes that page, and opens the list
of what is left. Removal from any surface does this, and a page opened for a project that is not there does the
same thing rather than rendering.

**There is no *Work* view.** It was a second copy of the tree's *Gaps* list plus a counter, in a third place,
behind one icon that meant five things at once — decided with the user. What replaced it:

- the sidebar's **Library** holds one expandable **Needs attention** row: a count line, the missing-file row, a
  stale row, an unowned row, then the faulty pages, then **More…**
- **More…** (or the row's title, or the row's title-bar button) opens **What needs doing** — a page with the room
  to say *why* each thing is listed and *where* the fix is: sources · faults · declared-but-missing · stale ·
  no owner · owed. A missing file is created from there with one click, which is the one write this application is allowed
- each mark means **one** thing: `⊗` a structural fault, `!` a declared page whose file is absent, `↺` the
  generator's stale verdict, `▲` an unowned page, `⇄` owed
- the **staleness report** moved to the Library's title bar, where the view it belonged to used to be

**Owed is never computed here.** *Documentation owed* is the product repository's own gate —
`scripts/docs-owed.mjs`, `npm run docs:owed`, waivers that expire (`11-review-and-cicd.md`) — so the page runs
that script as a task and reports what a run is: where, when, the exit code. Its output, in the terminal, is the
verdict, and the page says so. A second opinion about what is owed is the one thing a documentation platform
must not invent.

The selection is state, in one place (`extensions/temco-store/src/selection.ts`, in `workspaceState`): which project, and which page
of it you were last on. A project belongs to the window, not the machine — two windows on two products must not
answer about each other's pages. Opening a project makes it the one being worked on, and the library row, the
view's description and the dashboard all say so; a project that is removed while selected clears the selection
rather than leaving a view about something that is not there.

**On activation, nothing has to be clicked.** The one project with nothing chosen selects itself (with two or
more nothing is guessed — see *Surfaces*); the Workspace view arrives with the only repository's areas open and
their chapters beneath them; the Library opens *Projects*, so the names of what you have are on screen; and the
tab that opens is the welcome page (`temcoWorkbench.start.open`, default `welcome`), or — once that checkbox is off — the selected project's pages, then the page you were last on, then the overview.
A tree that re-decided itself after every refresh would be worse than one that never opened at all, so a
person's own expand or collapse is remembered for the session and beats both defaults — a refresh re-reads the
data, never the layout (`extensions/temco-workbench/src/views.ts`).

### The window

**As built$2** The figures below are the design; what exists is these same surfaces with different
names, and the difference is recorded here rather than left to be discovered:

- The sidebar holds **three** sections in one container, not two: **Workspace** (the page tree, the first thing built), **Work** (what needs doing — faults per open folder, and changes not yet shown), **Library** (the
  projects, the sources with their pages beneath them, and the catalog's own state).
- The unit is called a **project** in everything a person reads. The catalog's table is still `documents`; a
  project is what that row is, and renaming the table buys nothing but a migration.
- **Four pages, one job each.** The **hub** (`extensions/temco-projects/src/projectHub.ts`) is what one project *is* —
  Overview · Pages · Sources · AI · Builds, with Read and Build at the top; the list of projects is the Library's
  *Projects* row, not a page of its own. The **edit page**
  (`extensions/temco-projects/src/projectPage.ts`) is what it is *made of*. Opening the editor for every look at a project would make
  reading its pages and editing its fields the same act, so they are separate commands (`openProject` and
  `editProject`). The **dashboard** (`extensions/temco-workbench/src/home.ts`) is the application itself, and the **AI page**
  (`extensions/temco-ai/src/aiPage.ts`) is the AI. All four are tabs in one group.
- **The project's pages are tabs in one group**, and so are the documentation pages they open — never a split. The rule
  lives in `extensions/temco-ui/src/columns.ts` and is stated there once, together with the two wrong answers that led to it: a page
  opened `Beside` produced a second column a few hundred pixels wide, and "is the tab in front one of ours?" —
  tried twice — is not a question a webview tab answers reliably. So the file asks nothing about what is in
  front; it finds the group that already holds a page of ours and opens there, which makes one group the only
  outcome. `reveal` with that column also *moves* a page a previous layout left in a second group, so the split
  heals the next time the page is opened. `ViewColumn` is named in exactly one place in the whole source.
- **The layout survives a narrow column.** Every grid is `minmax(min(Nem, 100%), 1fr)`, every flex child that
  holds text is `min-width: 0`, and a table wider than the pane scrolls inside its own box. A bare `minmax(Nem,
  1fr)` sets a hard floor that pushed the right-hand column out of the pane — the page scrolled sideways
  instead of reflowing, and it looked like a rendering fault rather than a CSS one.
- The **AI page** (`extensions/temco-ai/src/aiPage.ts`) is the AI as something owned and configured — the four facts a person needs
  before trusting an answer, what can be downloaded, and which is chosen. It stands beside the AI *panel*: the
  panel is the conversation, the page is the setup. A project's AI tab carries the same facts plus the
  suggestions read from that project's pages.
- **Suggestions need no model.** The ones on the AI tab — an unowned page, a source never read, a chapter past
  about 25 pages, a repeated title — come from the catalog, so they are identical with the AI off. What a model
  adds is prose over the same facts, never the facts themselves.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Temco Platform — T3000 Developer Help                                     ⌘  ▢  ✕     │
├──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┤
│ ▣ Temco      │  Document — T3000 Developer Help  v1.4        [ Build ▾ ] │ TEMCO AI           │
│              │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │                    │
│              │  health 82 · 12 stale · 3 unowned · 0 broken links       │ You  summarise     │
│  DOCUMENTS   │  sources T3000Webview @ main · C:\docs\t3000-extra       │      this page     │
│   ▸ T3000    │  318 pages                                               │ AI   This page     │
│     Dev Help │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │      lists the API │
│   ▸ Webview  │  [ Pages ] [ Builds ] [ AI ] [ Settings ]                │      points; 14    │
│     Guide    │                                                          │      months old.   │
│   ▸ Firmware │   ▾ t3000/api-reference       13 pages  stale 4          │                    │
│     Help     │     ▾ data-points              5 pages  ok               │ [ Draft update ]   │
│              │       Data Points API          1 page   updated 2 days   │ [ Ask the area ]   │
│  SOURCES     │   ▾ t3000/architecture        37 pages  unowned          │ [ Models… ]        │
│   ● T3000    │                                                          │                    │
│     Webview  │                                                          │ local · offline    │
│   ● T3_Engine│                                                          │ qwen2.5-3b         │
│   ▸ C:\docs  │                                                          │                    │
│     \t3000-  │                                                          │                    │
│     extra    │                                                          │                    │
├──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┤
│ PROBLEMS  OUTPUT  TERMINAL  │  ! data-points.md   Order 20 is used twice                      │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 3 documents · 6 sources · 2 drafts to review · 14 pages owed        Markdown  Ln 3, Col 1     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**The AI goes on the right**, beside the page being worked on — that is where `app-layout.md`'s mock puts it,
and it is where a reader of a page wants a question answered.

**The AI sits in the right side bar, and that is a declaration, not a preference.** The container is contributed
under **`contributes.viewsContainers.secondarySidebar`** — a location VS Code's own schema has, and which this
design document wrongly claimed did not exist. The secondary side bar is therefore its default home: nothing to
drag, nothing to pick, no setup step. If someone moves it (the panel, the primary side bar), *Show the AI in the
right side bar* (`temcoAi.dockRight`, in the `temco-ai` module) focuses the view and invokes the per-container
`<containerId>.resetViewContainerLocation` command VS Code registers, which returns it to its declared location.
That is also why the extension never asks *where* a view currently is: the view-location API is not exposed to
extensions, and the reset command makes the question unnecessary.

### The toolbar

The design package puts the editor on the **website** — `15-online-editor.md`, *The editor core*, picks Monaco, *The screen* gives the toolbar
it needs, and *The API* the contract behind it. Two of those three are already true on the desktop without a line of ours, and
saying which is which is what keeps this small:

```
  what 15-online-editor.md asks for            the website has to      this application already has
  ──────────────────────────────────────────── ─────────────────────── ──────────────────────────────────
  Monaco, the editing core                      bundle it, ~2 MB        VS Code *is* Monaco plus a shell
  find, folding, breadcrumb outline, minimap    free once bundled       the same, from the editor
  gate findings as squiggles on the line        /api/docs/validate      src/diagnostics.ts, already written
  a toolbar of formatting buttons               build it in main.js     src/format.ts — this step
  link completion from index.json               a completion provider   Link to a page, a command
  a WYSIWYG mode                                refused on principle    refused for the same reason
  preview identical to the site                 docfx serve in an iframe the repository's own build
  publish, and the PR it opens                  POST /api/docs/publish  never: it builds, it does not publish
```

The one row that *is* work is the toolbar, and it lands in the editorial surfaces a person already uses rather
than in a page of ours: four buttons in the editor's title bar for a Markdown file, the same sixteen actions in
the submenu on the editor's context menu and on the tab's, and one **Format** entry that lists them all, so the
palette is the keyboard path. `15-online-editor.md`, *The screen* asks for the same list, and the set matches it: emphasis,
headings, the lists, the quote, the code block, the table, the rule, an image, a link to a page, an outline.

Three decisions inside it are worth recording, because each one had two answers:

| Decision | Taken | Why |
|---|---|---|
| The editor is VS Code's, with the project's toolbar on it | **not an embedded editor page** | the design package refuses an embedded VS Code for the website (`15-online-editor.md`, *What is not built*); here it would be embedding the editor inside the editor, and re-building the preview, the markers and the file handling that already exist |
| No Markdown formatting commands ship in VS Code | **so these are ours** | checked, not assumed: no `toggleBold` exists in any built-in extension, so `**…**` had no button before this |
| No keybindings are contributed | **deliberate** | `Ctrl+B` hides the side bar everywhere else, and this extension must keep working in an ordinary VS Code. Every action is a command named `temcoDocs.format.*`, so a chord is one binding away for whoever wants it |

The button that needed a rule rather than an implementation is **Link to a page**. `05-content-and-registry.md`
fixes the form — a *relative* `.md` link, resolved and warned on by the build, with `~/` for root-relative — so the
picker offers the pages the registry declares and inserts a path the gate can resolve. A page in another source is
refused in words, because no relative path reaches it. **Callout** takes its five kinds from DocFX's own alerts
(`> [!NOTE]`, `TIP`, `IMPORTANT`, `CAUTION`, `WARNING`), which is the syntax the renderer actually implements.

**An image is the one button that is not here**, and the reason is the write rule rather than the code: the file
has to be written into the page's own `media/` before the reference to it points at anything. That amendment, the
kinds of new document, and what each costs are the subject of `docs/editor-design.md`.

### Operating on things

The operations were in the sidebar's title bar — twelve buttons on the Library, four of them needing a target
nobody had picked — and the tree could only be read. What replaced it is one rule and one place per kind of thing:

```
  the title bar of a view        starts work, and stands for nothing in particular
  the ⋯ in that title bar        everything that needs a target, or is housekeeping
  a row                          operates on the thing the row is
  a page (dashboard, hub)        operates on the thing the page is about
```

```
 LIBRARY HEADER — before
 ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
 │ [+][▤][✓][◧][+⌄][⤓][⤓][●][⚙][☰][⌘][▶] │   12 buttons — and four of them have no target to act on
 └──────────────────────────────────────┘

 LIBRARY HEADER — after
 ┌──────────────────────────────┬───┐
 │ [+ New project] [Projects] [✓]│ ⋯ │   3 buttons, and the ⋯ holds everything else
 └──────────────────────────────┴───┘
   ⋯   Read every source · Read a source… · Add a source…
       Work on a project… · Build the project…
       Run a script… · Staleness report · Catalog · Set a token
```

The Workspace tree now carries the page operations, because that is where a page is. `page` and `missingPage` are
two context values rather than one, so the menu can offer *Create the missing page file* only where a file is
missing, and *Move* only where there is one.

```
 ▾ T3000  t3000 · 318 pages
   ▾ features  41 pages  ⇄ owed
       Trend Logs     ● 2 issues
       Data Points    ▲ unowned
       System Time    ⚠ missing file
       Trend Log      (read from the source)

   left click a row        a page opens · a folder expands          (the Explorer's own behaviour)
   right click a row       the row's menu — and the keyboard's menu key opens the same one
   on a folder row         New Page… · New Chapter… · New Page of a Kind ▸ · Reveal in Explorer
                           Open in Integrated Terminal · Copy Path · Copy Relative Path
                           Open the Registry
   on a page row           Open the Page · Reveal in Explorer · Open in Integrated Terminal
                           Copy Path · Copy Relative Path · Rename the Title…
                           Move the Page Up · Move the Page Down · Retire… · Open the Registry
   the view's toolbar      [New page] [New chapter] [Refresh] [⋯]  — the ⋯ is the list above, for the
                           row the tree last had focused
   drag a page onto        a chapter (chapter + order) · a sibling (two order values, swapped)
```

**The tree behaves the way the Explorer does, because that is the tree everyone already knows.** A left click opens
a page or expands a folder; a right click opens the row's own menu, which carries only what that row can do — a
page has no *New Page…*, a chapter has no *Rename*. Nothing is on hover, because a menu started from a row can only
ever be a quick pick in the middle of the top of the window, and the menu above is what replaces it. The one act
that is *not* the Explorer's is deliberate: **the view's `…` shows the same list for the row the tree last had
focused**, so the buttons in the title bar and the palette reach every act without a right click (a view title
action runs with the tree pane's context, and the row commands fall back to the view's selection).

**The kinds are the file types.** A page is always Markdown — its id is its path under `docs/`, the generator reads
its title from its first `#` — so the submenu lists what actually differs between one new page and another: the
headings it starts with (Article · Task · Reference · Troubleshooting · Release note).

**A drag is a registry write, and it says which values it will change.** Dropping a page on a chapter writes its
`chapter` and a new `order`, and the confirmation says the word *structure*, because `15-online-editor.md`, *Safety rules*, rule 4
marks that field as the docs owner's. Dropping it on a sibling in the same chapter swaps two `order` values and
nothing else. Nothing here renumbers a chapter: placing a page between two others is the generator's own job, and
the generator does it for whatever nobody curated.

**The projects page is the operations home**, which it already was — each card carries *Work on it · Open · Read
sources · Build · Edit · Scripts… · Remove…* — with one addition: **Scripts…** lists the repository's own
allowlisted scripts that *this* project's folders actually have, and runs the one you pick through the Library's
own command. A script belongs to a repository, so it is offered per project only because a project is what cites
one; when the project is not the one being worked on, it is made so first, out loud, because the Workspace view
changes with it.

### Documents — the home page

```

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Since you last looked
    ● T3000 Developer Help      12 pages changed · built 2 days ago · 2 drafts waiting
```
